The map
\theta_{B}
is defined as follows:

•

\theta_{B}
is the identity in the factors corresponding to
v\in\Sigma_{*}
.

•

\theta_{B}
is multiplication by
\sigma_{v}-1
(where
\sigma_{v}\in\mathfrak{g}_{v}\subset\mathfrak{g}
denotes Frobenius at
v
) in the factors corresponding to
v\in S^{\prime}-\Sigma
(see
[
rw
]
*Lemma 5).

•

For
v=\mathfrak{p}
and
{\Sigma_{*}}=\Sigma
, the map
\theta_{B}
on the component at
\mathfrak{p}
is projection
onto the first factor.

Since
\theta_{W}
is surjective, taking kernels in (
41
) yields a short exact sequence

(42)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\theta}_{\Sigma_{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\theta}_{\Sigma_{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\theta}_{\Sigma_{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Note that
B^{\theta}_{\Sigma}\cong\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{p}}]\oplus\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]^{\#S^{\prime}-1}
and
B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\theta}\cong\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]^{\#S^{\prime}-1}
. We define

(43)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\nabla}_{{\Sigma_{*}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)=\coker(f_{{\Sigma_{*}}}\colon V^{\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\theta}_{{\Sigma_{*}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\theta}_{\Sigma_{*}}).}

When
{\Sigma_{*}}=\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, the module
\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
is the same as the module

\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
defined in
[
dk
]
, and when we speak of it individually we will use the latter notation. However for
{\Sigma_{*}}=\Sigma
,
the module
\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
is a subquotient of the module
{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
defined in
loc. cit.
. We have introduced the
\tilde{\nabla}
notation so as to not conflict with the notation of
loc. cit.
, and so that we may speak of
{\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
and

\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
simultaneously when convenient, using the symbol
\tilde{\nabla}_{{\Sigma_{*}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
.

In view of (
37
) and (
42
), we have two exact sequences:

(44)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{L,{\Sigma_{*}},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\theta}_{\Sigma_{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\nabla}_{{\Sigma_{*}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}

(45)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cl_{\Sigma_{*}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\nabla}_{{\Sigma_{*}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\theta}_{\Sigma_{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

In
[
dk
]
we proved the following results. Write

(V^{\theta})_{R}=(V^{\theta})\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]}R,

and similarly with
V^{\theta}
replaced by any other
\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]
-module.

Lemma 4.1

(
[
dk
]
*Lemma A.4)
.

The module
(V^{\theta})_{R}
is free over
R
of rank equal to the rank of the free module
B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\theta}
, namely
\#S^{\prime}-1
. Hence the module

{\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{R}
is quadratically presented over
R
.

Theorem 4.2

(
[
dk
]
*Theorem 3.3)
.

We have

(46)

\Fitt_{R}(\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{R})=(\Theta^{L}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}).

We can now give the proof of Theorem
3.5
.

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

If we denote by
\{e_{v}\colon v\in S^{\prime}\}
the standard
R
-basis for

(B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}})_{R}=\prod_{v\in S^{\prime}}R,

then the module
(B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\theta})_{R}
has a basis

\{b_{v}=e_{v}-\theta_{B}(e_{v})e_{\infty}:v\in S^{\prime}-\infty\}

where
\infty\in S_{\infty}\subset S^{\prime}
is any fixed infinite place of
F
.
By Lemma
4.1
, the
R
-module
V^{\theta}_{R}
is free of rank
\#S^{\prime}-1
, and hence

\Fitt_{R}(\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{R})
is the ideal generated by the determinant of the square matrix
A

representing the map
f\colon V^{\theta}_{R}\longrightarrow(B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\theta})_{R}
with respect to any bases.

We choose any basis of
V^{\theta}_{R}
and the basis
\{b_{v}\colon v\in S^{\prime},v\neq\infty\}
for

(B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\theta})_{R}
.
The columns of the associated matrix
A
are indexed by the basis vectors
b_{v}
. For
v\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, the elements of the corresponding column vector lie, by definition, in the image of
\Ind_{\mathfrak{g}_{v}}^{\mathfrak{g}}\Delta\mathfrak{g}_{v}\longrightarrow R
.
This is exactly the ideal

I(v)=\ker(R\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{v}]^{-}).

The determinant of
A
therefore lies in
\prod_{v\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}I(v)
. In view of (
46
), the result follows.
∎

4.2.
Interpretation via Galois Cohomology

In
[
dk
]
*§A.3, we gave a description of the projection to the minus side of the class in
\Ext_{\mathbf{Z}[G]}^{1}(Y_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\theta},\Cl_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L))
determined by
{\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
via the exact sequence (
45
), using Galois cohomology. We recall this now and give the generalization that allows for the application to
\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
.

For each
v\in S^{\prime}
, let

G_{F,v}\cong{\rm Gal}(\overline{F}_{v}/F_{v})\subset G_{F}

denote the decomposition group at
v
associated to a place of
\overline{F}
above
v
restricting to the chosen place
w
of
L
. Let
M
be a
\mathfrak{g}
-module.
Suppose that we are given a 1-cocycle

\kappa\in Z^{1}(G_{F},M),

where
M
is endowed with a
G_{F}
-action via the canonical map
G_{F}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{g}.
Suppose that the restriction of
\kappa
to
{\rm Gal}(\overline{F}_{v}/L_{w}^{\nr})
is trivial. For a
\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]
-module
N
we write

N^{-}=(N\otimes\mathbf{Z}[1/2])/(c+1),

where
c\in\mathfrak{g}
denotes complex conjugation.
We may then define a
\mathfrak{g}
-module homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}\colon W_{v}^{-}=(\Ind_{\mathfrak{g}_{v}}^{\mathfrak{g}}W_{w}(L))^{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{-}}

by the rule

(47)

\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}(g\otimes(\sigma-1))=g\cdot\kappa(\sigma),\qquad g\in G,\ \ \sigma\in\WD(L_{w}^{\nr}/F_{v}).

The condition on
\kappa
ensures that
\kappa(\sigma)
is well-defined for
\sigma\in\WD(L_{w}^{\nr}/F_{v})
. It is elementary to check that
\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}
is well-defined, as follows.

•

If
g\in\mathfrak{g}_{v}
, then

\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}(1\otimes(g\sigma-g))=\kappa(g\sigma)-\kappa(g)=g\cdot\kappa(\sigma)=\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}(g\otimes(\sigma-1)).

•

If
\sigma\in\WD(L_{w}^{\nr}/F_{v})
and
\tau\in\WD(L_{w}^{\nr}/L_{w})
, then

\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}(1\otimes(\tau-1)(\sigma-1))=\kappa(\tau\sigma)-\kappa(\tau)-\kappa(\sigma)=0

since
\tau
acts trivially on
M
.

Write
Z_{v}=\Ind_{\mathfrak{g}_{v}}^{\mathfrak{g}}\Delta\mathfrak{g}_{v}
. Since
\Delta\mathfrak{g}_{v}
is canonically a
\mathfrak{g}_{v}
-module quotient of
W_{w}(L)
(see
34
),
Z_{v}^{-}
is canonically a
\mathfrak{g}
-module quotient of
W_{v}^{-}
.
If the restriction of
[\kappa]
to
{\rm Gal}(\overline{F}_{v}/L_{w})
is trivial, then
\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}
descends to a homomorphism

(48)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}\colon Z_{v}^{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{-}.}

Now let
M_{\Sigma_{*}}=\Cl_{{\Sigma_{*}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)^{-}
for
{\Sigma_{*}}=\Sigma
or
{\Sigma_{*}}=\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
. By class field theory, we may view
M_{\Sigma_{*}}
as the Galois group of a field extension
\tilde{L}_{\Sigma_{*}}/L
. The field
\tilde{L}_{\Sigma_{*}}
is the maximal abelian extension of
L
of odd order that is unramified outside
\Sigma^{\prime}
, tamely ramified at
\Sigma^{\prime}
, such that the primes in
{\Sigma_{*}}
split completely, and such that the conjugation action of the complex conjugation in
\mathfrak{g}
is inversion.
Let

\textstyle{\tilde{\lambda}_{\Sigma_{*}}\colon G_{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Gal}(\tilde{L}_{{\Sigma_{*}}}/L)\cong M_{\Sigma_{*}}}

denote the canonical homomorphism given by the reciprocity map of class field theory.
By
[
dk
]
*Lemma 6.3, the class
\tilde{\lambda}_{\Sigma_{*}}\in H^{1}(G_{L},M_{\Sigma_{*}})
is the restriction of a unique class

[\lambda_{\Sigma_{*}}]\in H^{1}(G_{F},M_{\Sigma_{*}}).

An explicit 1-coycle representing this class is given as follows. Let
\tilde{c}
denote a lift of the complex conjugation
c\in\mathfrak{g}
to an element of
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}={\rm Gal}(\tilde{L}_{\Sigma_{*}}/F)
. Then

(49)

\lambda_{\Sigma_{*}}(\tilde{\sigma})=\tilde{\lambda}_{\Sigma_{*}}(\tilde{\sigma}\tilde{c}\tilde{\sigma}^{-1}\tilde{c}^{-1})^{1/2},\qquad\tilde{\sigma}\in G_{F}.

Note that the cocycle
\lambda_{{\Sigma_{*}}}\in Z^{1}(G_{F},M_{\Sigma_{*}})
satisfies the condition described above for
\kappa,
namely that the restriction to

{\rm Gal}(\overline{F}_{v}/L_{w}^{\nr})
is trivial for each
v\in S^{\prime}
. This follows since
\tilde{L}_{\Sigma_{*}}\subset L_{w}^{\nr}
.
Furthermore if
v\in{\Sigma_{*}}
, then the restriction of
\lambda_{{\Sigma_{*}}}
to
{\rm Gal}(\overline{F}_{v}/L_{w})
is trivial.
Therefore the construction in (
47
) and (
48
) yields elements:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda_{{\Sigma_{*}}}}^{v}

\displaystyle\in\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]^{-}}(W_{v}^{-},M_{\Sigma_{*}}),\qquad v\in S^{\prime}-{\Sigma_{*}},

\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda_{{\Sigma_{*}}}}^{v}

\displaystyle\in\Hom_{\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]^{-}}(Z_{v}^{-},M_{\Sigma_{*}}),\qquad v\in{\Sigma_{*}}.

Lemma 4.3
.

The “snake map”
\varphi_{\Sigma_{*}}\colon(W_{\Sigma_{*}}^{\theta})^{-}\longrightarrow M_{\Sigma_{*}}
given by the minus part of the last nontrivial arrow in
(\ref{e:snake})
can be described explicitly by the formula

\varphi_{\Sigma_{*}}((a_{v})_{v\in S^{\prime}})=\sum_{v\in S^{\prime}}\varphi_{\lambda_{\Sigma_{*}}}^{v}(a_{v}).

Proof.

The proof is nearly identical to
[
dk
]
*Lemma A.9, but we give it for completeness and because of notational differences.
We use the description of the snake map given by Ritter and Weiss in
[
rw
]
*Theorem 5.
Write
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}={\rm Gal}(\tilde{L}/F)
, where as above
\tilde{L}
is the extension of
L
corresponding to
M_{\Sigma_{*}}
via class field theory.
Write
\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
for the augmentation ideal of
\mathbf{Z}[\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}]
and let
\Delta(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}},M_{\Sigma_{*}})
denote the kernel of the canonical projection

\mathbf{Z}[\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}]\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]
. There is a short exact sequence (see
[
rw
]
*Pg. 154)

(50)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\Sigma_{*}}\cong\dfrac{\Delta(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}},M_{\Sigma_{*}})}{\Delta(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}},M_{\Sigma_{*}})\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dfrac{\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}{\Delta(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}},M_{\Sigma_{*}})\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Let
v\in S^{\prime}
. The extension
\tilde{L}
is unramifed (over
L
) at
w
, so there is a canonical restriction map
\WD(L_{w}^{\nr}/F_{v})\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}.
In view of the definition of
W_{w}(L)
given in (
35
), this induces a canonical map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ind_{\mathfrak{g}_{v}}^{\mathfrak{g}}W_{w}(L)=\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}_{v}]}W_{w}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dfrac{\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}{\Delta(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}},M_{\Sigma})\Delta\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{v}}

In
[
rw
]
*Theorem 5, Ritter–Weiss show that the snake map is realized by

(51)

\varphi_{\Sigma_{*}}((a_{v})_{v\in S^{\prime}})=\sum_{v\in S^{\prime}}f_{v}(a_{v}).

Let
a=(a_{v})_{v\in S^{\prime}}\in(W_{\Sigma_{*}}^{\theta})^{-}
. Choose
v^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
such that the decomposition group
\mathfrak{g}_{v^{\prime}}\subset\mathfrak{g}
contains complex conjugation
c
(the existence of such a
v^{\prime}
is guaranteed by the assumptions on
S^{\prime}
).
Let
\tilde{c}
denote a lift of
c
to
\WD(L_{w^{\prime}}^{\nr}/F_{v})
.

For each
v\in S^{\prime}
, define
y_{v}\in W_{\Sigma_{*}}^{-}
to have component at
v
equal to
a_{v}
, component at
v^{\prime}
equal to

b_{v}=\theta_{W}(a_{v})\otimes(1-\tilde{c})/2\in(\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}_{v^{\prime}}]}W_{w^{\prime}}(L))^{-},

and all other components equal to 0. Then
a=\sum_{v\in S^{\prime}}y_{v}
since
\theta_{W}(a)=0
. Furthermore each
y_{v}
lies in
(W_{\Sigma_{*}}^{\theta})^{-}
by construction.
It therefore suffices to prove that
\varphi_{\Sigma_{*}}(y_{v})=\varphi_{\lambda_{\Sigma_{*}}}^{v}(a_{v})
for each
v\in S^{\prime}
. For this, we apply (
51
) with the tuple
(a_{v})_{v\in S^{\prime}}
replaced by
y_{v}
.

The module
W_{v}^{-}
is generated over
\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]^{-}
by elements of the form

a_{v}=\tilde{\sigma}-1\in I_{W}

for
\tilde{\sigma}\in\WD(L_{w}^{\nr}/F_{v})
.
Since we are working on the minus side, we have

\displaystyle f_{v}(a_{v})

\displaystyle=f_{v}((1-c)/2\otimes a_{v})=(1-\tilde{c})(\tilde{\sigma}-1)/2,

\displaystyle f_{v^{\prime}}(b_{v})

\displaystyle=(\tilde{\sigma}-1)(\tilde{c}-1)/2,

and hence

\varphi_{{\Sigma_{*}}}(y_{v})=(\tilde{\sigma}\tilde{c}-\tilde{c}\tilde{\sigma})/2.

Under the isomorphism noted in the first nonzero term in (
50
), this correponds to the element

(\tilde{\sigma}\tilde{c}\tilde{\sigma}^{-1}\tilde{c}^{-1})^{1/2}\in{\rm Gal}(\tilde{L}/L)\cong M_{\Sigma_{*}}.

By (
49
), this is precisely the value of
\varphi_{\lambda_{{\Sigma_{*}}}}^{v}(\tilde{\sigma}-1)=\lambda_{{\Sigma_{*}}}(\tilde{\sigma})
.
∎

As we now describe, the Galois theoretic description of
\nabla_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)^{-}
provided by Lemma
4.3
yields an explicit method of constructing homomorphisms

\nabla_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)^{-}\longrightarrow B

for
\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]^{-}
-modules
B
. Recall
R=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\mathfrak{g}]^{-}
.
We work over an arbitrary
R
-algebra
A
since this is the context we will require later.
Therefore let
B
denote an
A
-module and let
[\kappa]\in H^{1}(G_{F},B)
denote a Galois cohomology class, where
B
is endowed with a
G_{F}
-action via the composition

G_{F}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow A^{*}.

Suppose that:

•

The class
[\kappa]
is unramified outside
\Sigma^{\prime}
, locally trivial at
\Sigma
, and tamely ramified at
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

•

Let
B_{0}\subset B
denote the
A
-submodule generated by the image of the restriction

[\kappa]_{|G_{L}}\in H^{1}(G_{L},B)=\Hom_{\cont}(G_{L},B).

The module

B/B_{0}
is generated over
A
by the images of elements
x_{v}\in B
for
v\in\Sigma
and elements
x_{\mathfrak{p}},x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\in B
. The element
x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
is fixed by the action of
G_{F,\mathfrak{p}}
(i.e. by the action of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{p}}
).

•

The class
[\kappa]
is represented by a 1-cocycle
\kappa
satisfying the following.

–

For
v\in\Sigma
and
\sigma\in G_{F,v}
, we have
\kappa(\sigma)=x_{v}(\sigma-1).

–

For
\sigma\in\WD(\overline{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}/F_{\mathfrak{p}})
, we have

(52)

\kappa(\sigma)=(\sigma-1)x_{\mathfrak{p}}+\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\sigma)x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime},

where
\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}
is as in (
39
).

–

There exists
\tau\in G_{F}
, a lift of the complex conjugation in
\mathfrak{g}
, such that for all
\sigma\in G_{F}
we have

(53)

\kappa(\sigma)=[\kappa]|_{G_{L}}({\sigma}\tau{\sigma}^{-1}\tau^{-1})/2\in B_{0}.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
B
be an
A
-module and
[\kappa]\in H^{1}(G_{F},B)
a Galois cohomology class satsifying the three bulleted points above.
Write
A_{\mathfrak{p}}=A\otimes_{R}\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{p}}]^{-}
. There is a surjective
A
-module homomorphism

(54)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}\colon\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

induced by the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\colon(B_{\Sigma})_{A}\cong(A\oplus A_{\mathfrak{p}})\oplus A^{\#S^{\prime}-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

defined as follows:

•

For
a_{v}\in A
in the component at
v\in S^{\prime}-\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have
\alpha(a_{v})=a_{v}\kappa(\sigma_{v})
, where
\sigma_{v}
denotes the Frobenius element at
v
.

•

For
a_{v}\in A
in the component at
v\in\Sigma
, we have
\alpha(a_{v})=a_{v}x_{v}
.

•

For
(a_{v},b_{v})\in A\oplus A_{\mathfrak{p}}
in the component at
\mathfrak{p}
, we have
\alpha(a_{v},b_{v})=a_{v}x_{\mathfrak{p}}+b_{v}x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}.

Proof.

The homomorphism
[\kappa]_{|G_{L}}\in H^{1}(G_{L},B)=\Hom_{\cont}(G_{L},B)
is unramified outside
\Sigma^{\prime}
, locally trivial at
\Sigma
, and tamely ramified at
\Sigma^{\prime}
. It follows from class field theory that
[\kappa]_{|G_{L}}
factors through

M_{\Sigma}\cong{\rm Gal}(\tilde{L}_{\Sigma}/L)

and therefore induces a surjective map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M_{\Sigma})_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}.}

Equation (
53
) implies that
\kappa
takes values in
B_{0}
.

To prove that
\alpha
induces a map
\alpha_{1}
as in (
54
), it suffices to prove that the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W_{\Sigma}^{\theta})_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B_{\Sigma}^{\theta})_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B_{\Sigma})_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

can be factored as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(W_{\Sigma}^{\theta})_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M_{\Sigma})_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\Sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa|_{G_{L}}}

For then the image of
(V^{\theta})_{A}
in
(W_{\Sigma}^{\theta})_{R}
vanishes under
\alpha
. Note that the composition
\kappa|_{G_{L}}\circ\varphi_{\Sigma}
equals
\varphi_{\kappa}:=\sum_{v\in S^{\prime}}\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}
, by Lemma
4.3
together with equations (
49
) and (
53
).

The fact that the restriction of
\alpha
to
(W_{\Sigma}^{\theta})_{A}
equals
\varphi_{\kappa}
follows from the assumptions on
\kappa
, as we now check on each component.

•

Any
v\in S^{\prime}-\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
in unramifed in
L
and hence
(W_{v})_{A}
is generated over
A
by

\sigma_{v}-1
, where
\sigma_{v}
is the Frobenius element at
v
.
By definition of the map
W_{\Sigma}\longrightarrow B_{\Sigma}
, the image of
\sigma_{v}-1
in the component of
B_{\Sigma}
at
v
is simply 1. Therefore

\alpha(\sigma_{v}-1)=\kappa(\sigma_{v})=\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}(\sigma_{v}-1).

•

For
v\in\Sigma
, let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{g}_{v}
, and consider the element
\sigma-1
in the
v
-component of
(B_{\Sigma})_{A}
. We find:

\alpha(\sigma-1)=x_{v}(\sigma-1)=\kappa(\sigma)=\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}(\sigma-1).

•

Let
y\in(W_{\mathfrak{p}})_{R}
be represented by
\tilde{\sigma}-1
for
{\sigma}\in\WD(L_{w}^{\nr}/F_{\mathfrak{p}})
.
Consider
j_{\mathfrak{p}}(y)
in the
\mathfrak{p}
-component of
(B_{\Sigma})_{R}
. We find:

\displaystyle\alpha(j_{\mathfrak{p}}(y))

\displaystyle=\alpha(\sigma|_{L_{w}}-1,\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}({\sigma}))

\displaystyle=(\sigma-1)x_{\mathfrak{p}}+\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}({\sigma})x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\kappa(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\kappa}^{v}(y).

This concludes the proof that
\alpha
induces the desired map
\alpha_{1}
. In fact, if we let
\alpha_{0}
denote the composition
of
\alpha
with the projection
B\longrightarrow B/B_{0}
, then we have demonstrated a commutative diagram

(55)

The surjectivity of
\alpha_{0}
follows by the assumption that
B/B_{0}
is generated over
R
by the
x_{v}
for
v\in\Sigma
along with
x_{\mathfrak{p}},x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
.
The surjectivity of
\alpha_{1}
then follows from the Five Lemma.
∎

4.3.
The module
\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}

Recall
R=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\mathfrak{g}]^{-},\overline{R}=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[G]^{-}
.
As we did with
(B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\theta})_{R}
in the proof of Theorem
3.5
, we can write down a generating set for the
R
-module
(B_{\Sigma}^{\theta})_{R}
as follows. The module
(B_{\Sigma})_{R}
is generated over
R
by the vectors
e_{0}=(1,0)
and
e_{1}=(0,1)
in the component
R\oplus R_{\mathfrak{p}}
at
\mathfrak{p}
together with the standard basis vectors
e_{v}
for
\prod_{v\in S^{\prime}\setminus\mathfrak{p}}R\subset(B_{\Sigma})_{R}
.
The module
(B_{\Sigma}^{\theta})_{R}
is then generated by

\{b_{0}=e_{0}-e_{\infty},\ b_{1}=e_{1},\ b_{v}=e_{v}-\theta_{B}(e_{v})e_{\infty}\colon v\in S^{\prime}\setminus\{\infty,\mathfrak{p}\}\},

where
\infty\in S_{\infty}\subset S^{\prime}
is any fixed infinite place of
F
.

Denote the image of the vectors
b_{0}
and
b_{1}
in
\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
by
\overline{b}_{0}
and
\overline{b}_{1}
, respectively. Recall the
R
-algebra
R_{\mathscr{L}}
defined in (
31
). We define

(56)

\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}=\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{R}\otimes_{R}R_{\mathscr{L}}/(\overline{b}_{1}+{\mathscr{L}}\overline{b}_{0}).

The following is the analogue of Theorem
4.4
for the module
\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}
. Again we work over an arbitrary
R
-algebra
A
and write
A_{\mathscr{L}}=R_{\mathscr{L}}\otimes_{R}A
,
\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},A}=\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}\otimes_{R}A
.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
B
and
[\kappa]\in H^{1}(G_{F},B)
be as in Theorem
4.4
. Suppose that
\tilde{B}
is an
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}
-module endowed with an
A
-module map
B\longrightarrow\tilde{B}
such that the image of
B
generates
\tilde{B}
over
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}
. Suppose further that the images in
\tilde{B}
of
x_{\mathfrak{p}},x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\in B
defined in (
52
) satisfy

x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}+{\mathscr{L}}\cdot x_{\mathfrak{p}}=0
. Then the map
\alpha_{1}
of
(\ref{e:alpha1})
induces a surjective
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}
-module homomorphism

(57)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{{\mathscr{L}}}\colon\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{B}.}

Proof.

Since the image of
B
generates
\tilde{B}
as an
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}
-module, the surjection
\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{A}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow B
induces a surjection

(58)

\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{A}\otimes_{A}A_{{\mathscr{L}}}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\tilde{B}.

Since
\alpha_{1}(\overline{b}_{0})=x_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\alpha_{1}(\overline{b}_{1})=x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
, the equality
x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}+{\mathscr{L}}\cdot x_{\mathfrak{p}}=0
implies that the surjection (
58
) factors through

\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},A}=\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{A}\otimes_{A}A_{{\mathscr{L}}}/(\overline{b}_{1}+{\mathscr{L}}\overline{b}_{0})

as desired.
∎

4.4.
Gross’s Conjecture via Fitting Ideals

In this section we prove the following interpretation of Gross’s Conjecture.

Theorem 4.6
.

The
R_{\mathscr{L}}
-module
\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}
is quadratically presented and we have

\Fitt_{R_{\mathscr{L}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}})=(\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})-\Theta_{L}).

Therefore, the equality

\Fitt_{R_{\mathscr{L}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}})=0

implies the
p
-part of the modified Gross conjecture:

\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})\equiv\Theta_{L}\pmod{I^{2}}.

We would like to point out that most of the computations of §
4.4
are the same as, or slight variants of, the calculations of Burns, Kurihara, and Sano in §5 of
[
bks
]
. Our approach to relating the Brumer–Stark unit
u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}
to Ritter–Weiss modules and studying its properties is modeled after theirs. Our innovation is the definition of
R_{\mathscr{L}}
and
\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}
and the application of these techniques to the statement and proof of Theorem
4.6
.

First we note that the same construction used to define
\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)=\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
above, but working over
H
rather than
L
, gives rise to modules

\nabla_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(H)
and
\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(H)
.
Since
H/F
is unramified outside
\Sigma\cup\Sigma^{\prime}
, these modules
are the cokernels of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\theta}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\theta}(H),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{H,\Sigma}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\theta}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\theta}(H),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}}

respectively, with the
same
domain and codomain.
The modules
\nabla_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(H)_{\overline{R}}
and
\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(H)_{\overline{R}}
satisfy properties analogous to those stated in Lemma
4.1
and Theorem
1.2
. Specifically,
V^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}
is free over
\overline{R}
with constant rank equal to the rank of the free module
B^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}
, namely
\#S^{\prime}-1
. Furthermore, as stated in Theorem
4.2
above,
we have by
[
dk
]
*Theorem 3.3 the equalities

\Fitt_{\overline{R}}\nabla_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(H)_{\overline{R}}=(\Theta_{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{H}),\qquad\Fitt_{\overline{R}}\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(H)_{\overline{R}}=(\Theta_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{H})=0.

[

]

To give the analogous diagram for
\Sigma
, we need some additional notation.

\overline{B}_{\Sigma}(L)=\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{p}}]\oplus\bigoplus_{v\in S^{\prime}\setminus\mathfrak{p}}\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}],

and let

be the projection in which the first component at
\mathfrak{p}
, which is a factor
\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]
, has been forgotten.

defined in (
43
).
Let

. Note that since
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\Gamma
, multiplication by
\mathrm{N}\Gamma
induces a well-defined map

\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{p}}]\longrightarrow\mathrm{N}\Gamma\cdot\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]=\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]^{\Gamma}

and hence a well-defined map
\overline{B}_{\Sigma}(L)\longrightarrow B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}(L)^{\Gamma}
. By
[

]
*Lemma B.2, we then have a commutative diagram:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.80664pt}{-15.8109pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}

Write
t=\#S^{\prime}-1
and fix an
R
-basis
\{v_{1},v_{2},\dotsc,v_{t}\}
of
V^{\theta}(L)_{R}
.
As in the proof of Theorem
3.5
, we choose the following basis for
B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\theta}(L)_{R}
:

(61)

\{b_{v}=e_{v}-\theta_{B}(e_{v})e_{\infty}:v\in S^{\prime}-\infty\},

where
\infty\in S_{\infty}\subset S^{\prime}
is any fixed infinite place of
F
and
\{e_{v}\}
is the standard basis of

B_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}(L)_{R}=\prod_{v\in S^{\prime}}R.

Let
\{\overline{v}_{1},\dotsc,\overline{v}_{t}\}

and
\{\overline{b}_{v}\}

denote the
\overline{R}
-bases of
V^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}
and

B^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}
, respectively, obtained by applying the horizontal maps in (
59
).

Having fixed these bases, we define:

•

\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}\in M_{t\times t}(R)
is the matrix for
f_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

•

\euscr{A}_{\Sigma}
is the
t\times(t+1)
matrix representing the map
f_{\Sigma}
, with second column having entries in
R_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{p}}]^{-}
and all other columns having entries in
R
.

By the commutative diagrams (
59
) and (
60
) we have:

•

The reduction of
\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
modulo
I
, denoted

\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}\in M_{t\times t}(\overline{R})
, is the matrix for
f_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

•

Let
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma}
denote the matrix in
M_{t\times t}(\overline{R})
obtained from
\euscr{A}_{\Sigma}
by deleting the first column and reducing the other entries modulo
I
.
Then
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma}
is the matrix for
f_{H,\Sigma}
.

We furthermore note that:

•

The matrices
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma}
agree other than their first columns, since the components away from
\mathfrak{p}
of the maps
f_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}},f_{H,\Sigma}
are the same.

•

The first column of
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
consists of all zeroes, since
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\Gamma
.

We now define a square
t\times t
matrix
\euscr{A}_{V}
. The last
t-1
columns of
\euscr{A}_{V}
have entries in

\overline{R}
and are equal to the last
t-1
columns of
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
(equivalently,

\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma}
). The first column of
\euscr{A}_{V}
is the column vector
(\overline{v}_{i})_{i=1}^{t}
, with entries in
V^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}
. It makes sense to consider the determinant of
\euscr{A}_{V}
as an element of
V^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}
by Leibniz formula for determinants.

Lemma 4.7
.

We have
\det(\euscr{A}_{V})\in\ker(f_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}).

Proof.

The value
f_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\det(\euscr{A}_{V}))
is the determinant of the matrix in which the first column of
\euscr{A}_{V}
has been replaced by column whose elements are
f_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\overline{v}_{i})\in B^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}.

With respect to the decomposition of
B^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}
as a product over the places
v\in S^{\prime}
,
each component of
(f_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\overline{v}_{i}))_{i=1}^{t}
corresponding to a place
v\in S^{\prime}\setminus\mathfrak{p}
is equal to another column of the matrix, namely the column corresponding to
v
. Meanwhile the component at
v=\mathfrak{p}
is the 0 vector, as noted in the bulleted point above, regarding
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
. It follows that the determinant is 0 in every component of
B^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}.

∎

From the exact sequence

(62)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{O}_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*})_{\overline{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(H)_{\overline{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}}

it follows from Lemma
4.7
that
\det(\euscr{A}_{V})\in V^{\theta}(H)_{\overline{R}}
is the image of a unit

(63)

\epsilon\in(\mathcal{O}_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*})_{\overline{R}}.

Lemma 4.8
.

We have
\epsilon\in(\mathcal{O}_{H,\mathfrak{p},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*})_{\overline{R}}
.

Proof.

Let
v\in\Sigma
and let
w
be the chosen place of
H
above
v
in the definition of
V(H)
.
Recall the short exact sequence

(64)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ind_{G_{w}}^{G}\mathcal{O}_{w}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{v}(H)=\Ind_{G_{w}}^{G}V_{w}(H_{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{v}(H)=\Ind_{G_{w}}^{G}W_{w}(H_{w}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{v}}

To show that
\epsilon\in(\mathcal{O}_{H,\mathfrak{p},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*})_{\overline{R}}
, we must show that the component of
\det(\euscr{A}_{V})
in
V_{v}(H)
has vanishing image in
W_{v}(H)
under
\omega_{v}
.
Now
\omega_{v}(\det(\euscr{A}_{V}))
is the determinant of the matrix
\euscr{A}_{\omega_{v}}
in which the first column of
\euscr{A}_{V}
has been replaced by
(\omega_{v}(\overline{v}_{i}))_{i=1}^{t}
.
We use the description of
W_{w}(H_{w})
given by Ritter–Weiss in
[
rw
]
*§3. We have

(65)

W_{w}(H_{w})=\{(x,y)\in\Delta G_{w}\oplus\mathbf{Z}[G_{w}/I_{w}]\colon\overline{x}=(\sigma_{w}-1)y\}.

Here
\sigma_{w}\in G_{w}/I_{w}
denotes Frobenius.
If we write

\omega_{v}(\overline{v}_{i})=\sum_{\sigma\in G/G_{w}}\sigma\otimes(x_{\sigma,i},y_{\sigma,i}),

then
\euscr{A}_{\omega_{v}}
has first column equal to these values,
and another column (the column corresponding to
v
) equal to

\sum_{\sigma\in G/G_{w}}\sigma\otimes x_{\sigma,i}\in\Ind_{G_{w}}^{G}\Delta G_{w}.

Indeed, this is the component of
f_{H,\Sigma}
at the factor of
B^{\theta}(H)
corresponding to
v
. Because of the relationship between
\overline{x}
and
y
in (
65
), it is easy to see that the determinant of such a matrix is zero. This is clear for the first component of the ordered pair, and for the second we note that

(\sigma_{w}-1)y_{\sigma,i}=\overline{x}_{\sigma,i}.

The vanishing of
\det\euscr{A}_{\omega_{v}}
yields the desired result
\epsilon\in(\mathcal{O}_{H,\mathfrak{p},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*})_{\overline{R}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
denote the chosen place of
H
above
\mathfrak{p}
used in the definition of
V(H)
. We have

\ord_{G}(\epsilon):=\sum_{\sigma\in G}\ord_{\mathfrak{P}}(\sigma(\epsilon))\sigma^{-1}=\det(\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma})=x\Theta_{H}

for some unit
x\in\overline{R}^{*}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the previous calculations. The key point here is that since
\mathfrak{p}
splits completely in
H
, we have
H_{\mathfrak{P}}=F_{\mathfrak{p}}
, hence
V_{\mathfrak{P}}(H_{\mathfrak{P}})\cong H_{\mathfrak{P}}^{*}
and
W_{\mathfrak{P}}(H_{\mathfrak{P}})\cong\mathbf{Z}
. The sequence (
64
)
becomes the canonical sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ind_{1}^{G}\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{P}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)=\Ind_{1}^{G}H_{\mathfrak{P}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)=\Ind_{1}^{G}\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{Z}[G]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}}

with
\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}=1\otimes\ord_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
The composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Z}[G]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}}

therefore is precisely the map
\ord_{G}
. It follows that the value of
\ord_{G}(\epsilon)
is the determinant of the matrix in which the first column of
\euscr{A}_{V}
has been replaced by
\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}(v_{i})
. But this is by definition the matrix
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma}
.

To conclude, we note that

(\det(\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma}))=\Fitt_{\overline{R}}\nabla_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(H)=(\Theta_{H})

by
[
dk
]
*Theorem 3.3 and Corollary 6.2.
∎

If follows from Lemma
4.9
that

(66)

\epsilon=x\cdot u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}.

For the next lemma, recall from §
1.2
that we have a map

\rec_{G}\colon(\mathcal{O}_{H,\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*})_{\overline{R}}\longrightarrow I/I^{2},\qquad\epsilon\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\in G}(\rec_{\mathfrak{P}}(\sigma(\epsilon))-1)\tilde{\sigma}^{-1},

where
\tilde{\sigma}
is a lift of
\sigma
in
\mathfrak{g}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

We have

\rec_{G}(\epsilon)\equiv\det(\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}})\text{ in }I/I^{2}.

Proof.

Recall from the proof of Lemma
4.9
that
V_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)=\Ind_{1}^{G}H_{\mathfrak{P}}^{*}
. The homomorphism

\rec_{\mathfrak{P}}\colon H_{\mathfrak{P}}^{*}\longrightarrow\Gamma\cong I_{\Gamma}/I_{\Gamma}^{2}

therefore induces a map that we again denote
\rec_{\mathfrak{P}}\colon V_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)\longrightarrow I/I^{2}.

The composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{H,\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\mathfrak{p}}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I/I^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rec_{\mathfrak{P}}}

is precisely the map
\rec_{G}
. It follows that
\rec_{G}(\epsilon)
is the determinant of the matrix
\euscr{A}_{\rec}
in which the first column of
\euscr{A}_{V}
has been replaced by
(\rec_{\mathfrak{P}}(\overline{v}_{i}))_{i=1}^{t}
.
We must prove that

\det(\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}})\equiv\det(\euscr{A}_{\rec})\pmod{I^{2}}.

To prove this claim, first note that
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
\euscr{A}_{\rec}
have all columns after the first equal in
\overline{R}
. It therefore suffices to show that the first columns of
\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
\euscr{A}_{\rec}
are equal in
I/I^{2}
.

This follows from an unwinding of the definitions. We revisit the definition of
V_{\mathfrak{P}}(H_{\mathfrak{P}})
given in (
35
). In this notation, the map

\rec_{\mathfrak{P}}\colon V_{\mathfrak{P}}(H_{\mathfrak{P}})\longrightarrow I_{\Gamma}/I_{\Gamma}^{2}

giving the first column of
\euscr{A}_{\rec}
is simply induced by the canonical restriction map

\sigma-1\mapsto\sigma|_{L}-1,\qquad\sigma\in\WD(H_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\text{ab}}/F_{\mathfrak{p}}).

Let
w
denote the chosen place of
L
above
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{p}
.
By (
40
), the map
V_{\mathfrak{P}}(L_{\mathfrak{P}})\longrightarrow I
giving the first column of
\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}

is also induced by the restriction
\sigma-1\mapsto\sigma|_{L}-1
for
\sigma\in\WD(L_{w}^{\text{ab}}/F_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

To conclude, we observe that by
[
dk
]
*Lemma B.1, the composition of the maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ind_{\mathfrak{g}_{v}}^{\mathfrak{g}}V_{w}(L_{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Ind_{\mathfrak{g}_{v}}^{\mathfrak{g}}V_{w}(L_{w}))^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ind_{1}^{G}V_{\mathfrak{P}}(H_{\mathfrak{P}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{N}\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

is induced by the map
V_{w}(L_{w})\longrightarrow V_{\mathfrak{P}}(H_{\mathfrak{P}})
given by restriction:
\sigma-1\mapsto\sigma|_{H_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\text{ab}}}-1.

∎

We are finally ready for:

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

Define

V_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\theta}=V^{\theta}(L)\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]}R_{{\mathscr{L}}},\qquad B_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\theta}=B_{\Sigma}^{\theta}(L)\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[\mathfrak{g}]}R_{{\mathscr{L}}}/(b_{1}+{\mathscr{L}}b_{0})\cong(R_{{\mathscr{L}}})^{t}.

The module
B_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\theta}
has free generators
\overline{b}_{0},\overline{b}_{2},\overline{b}_{3},\dotsc,\overline{b}_{t}
over
R_{{\mathscr{L}}}
. The module
V_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\theta}
has free generators
v_{1},\dotsc,v_{t}
over
R_{{\mathscr{L}}}
.
Therefore

\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}=\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}[G]}R_{{\mathscr{L}}}/(\overline{b}_{1}+{\mathscr{L}}\overline{b}_{0})

has a quadratic
R_{{\mathscr{L}}}
-module presentation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{{\mathscr{L}}}}

By definition, the matrix
\euscr{A}_{{\mathscr{L}}}
for
f_{{\mathscr{L}}}
with respect to our chosen bases is the matrix

\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
with the first column replaced by the first column of

\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}-{\mathscr{L}}\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma}.
Note that this first column has entries in the ideal
(I,{\mathscr{L}})\subset R_{{\mathscr{L}}}
that is annihilated by
I
. Furthermore
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma}
have columns after the first that are equal. It follows that

\det(\euscr{A}_{{\mathscr{L}}})=\det(\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}})-{\mathscr{L}}\det(\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma}).

By Lemma
4.9
, we have
\det(\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma})=x\Theta_{H}
for some
x\in\overline{R}^{*}
. By (
66
) and Lemma
4.10
, we have

\det(\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}})=x\cdot\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})
in
I/I^{2}
, with the same
x
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\det(\euscr{A}_{{\mathscr{L}}})

\displaystyle=\det(\euscr{A}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}})-{\mathscr{L}}\det(\overline{\euscr{A}}_{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=x\cdot\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})-x\cdot{\mathscr{L}}\Theta_{H}

\displaystyle=x\cdot(\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})-\Theta_{L}).

Since
x
is a unit, the equality

\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}})=(\det(\euscr{A}_{\mathscr{L}}))=(\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})-\Theta_{L})

follows.
Since

\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})-\Theta_{L}\in I/I^{2},

the second statement of the theorem follows from the first by Theorem
3.4
.
∎

4.5.
Working componentwise

The rings
R
and
\overline{R}
are not in general connected.
In working with modular forms, it will be convenient to replace these rings with individual components.
We will also need to extend scalars when working with Galois representations, so we do so already at this point. Therefore let
E
denote a finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
and let
\mathcal{O}
denote the ring of integers of
E
. We assume that
E
contains the image of every character of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Write
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{p}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, where
\mathfrak{g}_{p}
is the
p
-Sylow subgroup of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
is the subgroup of
\mathfrak{g}
containing the elements of prime-to-
p
order. For each odd character
\psi
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, let
R_{\psi}
denote the group ring
\mathcal{O}[\mathfrak{g}_{p}]
endowed with the
\mathfrak{g}
-action in which
g=g_{p}\cdot g^{\prime}
(with
g_{p}\in\mathfrak{g}_{p},g^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
) acts by multiplication by
g_{p}\psi(g^{\prime})
.
We then have an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}[\mathfrak{g}]
-algebras

{R}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}[\mathfrak{g}]^{-}\cong\prod_{\psi}R_{\psi},

where the product ranges over the odd characters
\psi
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
.
We let

R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi}=R_{\mathscr{L}}\otimes_{R}R_{\psi},\qquad\qquad\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\psi}=\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}\otimes_{R_{\mathscr{L}}}R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi}.

We consider the analogous decomposition

G=G_{p}\times G^{\prime}
. If
\chi
is an odd character of
G^{\prime}
,
we let
\overline{R}_{\chi}
denote the group ring
\mathcal{O}[G_{p}]
endowed with the
G
-action in which
g=g_{p}\cdot g^{\prime}
acts by multiplication by
g_{p}\chi(g^{\prime})
. Then
\overline{R}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}\cong\prod_{\chi}\overline{R}_{\chi}
with the product running over the odd characters
\chi
of
G^{\prime}
.

If
\chi
is an odd character of
G^{\prime}
, then it may be viewed as a character of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
via the canonical projection
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\longrightarrow G^{\prime}
, and we may consider both
R_{\chi}
and
\overline{R}_{\chi}
. Furthermore, in this case if we let
I_{\chi}
denote the image of the relative augmentation ideal
I
in
R_{\chi}
, then we have
R_{\chi}/I_{\chi}\cong\overline{R}_{\chi}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

The equality

(67)

\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi})=0

for each odd character
\chi
of
G^{\prime}
implies the equality

(68)

\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}})=0.

Proof.

As
\mathcal{O}
is free (and hence faithfully flat) over
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
, in order to prove (
68
) it suffices to show that

\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O})=0.

As

R_{{\mathscr{L}}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}\cong\prod_{\psi}R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi},

with the product running over all odd characters
\psi
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, it suffices to prove that

(69)

\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\psi})=0

for all such
\psi
.
The assumption (
67
) is precisely this result if
\psi
factors through
G^{\prime}
.

It therefore remains to show that (
69
) holds if
\psi
is an odd character of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
that does not factor through
G^{\prime}
. For such
\psi
, there exists
\sigma
in the kernel of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\longrightarrow G^{\prime}
such that
\psi(\sigma)\neq 1
. Since
\psi
has prime-to-
p
order, the element
1-\psi(\sigma)
is a unit in
\mathcal{O}
. It follows that the image of
1-\sigma\in I
in
R_{\psi}
is a unit. Since
{\mathscr{L}}I=0
in
R_{{\mathscr{L}}}
, it follows that the image of
{\mathscr{L}}
in
R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi}
vanishes, and hence that

(70)

R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi}\cong R_{\psi}/(\Theta_{L},I^{2}).

In view of the definition (
56
), and again applying
{\mathscr{L}}=0
in
R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi}
, we find

\displaystyle\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\psi}

\displaystyle\cong(\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi}})/(\overline{b}_{1})

\displaystyle\cong\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi}},

since
\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)/\overline{b}_{1}\cong\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)
by the definitions of these modules.
Now

\Fitt_{R}(\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{R})=(\Theta_{L})

by
[
dk
]
*Theorem 3.3 as stated in Theorem
4.2
above.
The desired result (
69
) follows since
\Theta_{L}=0
in
R_{{\mathscr{L}},\psi}
by (
70
).
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\chi
be an odd character of
G^{\prime}
and let
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},K\subset G^{\prime}
denote the kernels of
\chi

when viewed as characters of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
, respectively. Let
R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}^{\prime}
and
\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi}^{\prime}
denote the ring
R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}
and the module
\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi}
, respectively, defined using the fields
L^{\mathfrak{K}}
and
H^{K}
in place of
L
and
H
. Then there exist canonical isomorphisms

R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}^{\prime}\cong R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi},\qquad\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi}^{\prime}\cong\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi}.

Proof.

There is clearly an
\mathcal{O}[\mathfrak{g}]
-algebra isomorphism
R_{\chi}^{\prime}\cong R_{\chi}
, as these are both the group ring
\mathcal{O}[\mathfrak{g}_{p}]
in which
g=g_{p}g^{\prime}
acts via
\chi(g^{\prime})
. The elements
\Theta_{L^{\mathfrak{K}}}
and
\Theta_{L}
correspond under this isomorphism. Similarly we have an isomorphism
\overline{R}_{\chi}^{\prime}\cong\overline{R}_{\chi}
under which
\Theta_{H^{K}}
and
\Theta_{H}
correspond.
The
\mathcal{O}[\mathfrak{g}]
-algebra isomorphism
R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}^{\prime}\cong R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}
follows immediately from these considerations.

For the second isomorphism of the lemma, we first show that

(71)

\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)_{R_{\chi}}\cong\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L^{\mathfrak{K}})_{R_{\chi}}.

For this, we note that by
[
dk
]
*Lemma B.1, there is an isomorphism

V^{\theta}(L^{\mathfrak{K}})\cong(\mathrm{N}{\mathfrak{K}})V^{\theta}(L)\subset V^{\theta}(L),

yielding a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\theta}(L^{\mathfrak{K}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\theta}_{\Sigma}(L^{\mathfrak{K}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L^{\mathfrak{K}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\theta}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\theta}_{\Sigma}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\nabla}_{\Sigma}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{K}}

Upon tensoring with
R_{\chi}
, the arrow labelled
\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{K}
becomes an isomorphism. Indeed,
\mathfrak{K}
acts trivially on
R_{\chi}
, whence
\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{K}
acts as
\#\mathfrak{K}
, which is prime-to-
p
and hence invertible in
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. The isomorphism (
71
) follows.

The desired isomorphism
\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi}^{\prime}\cong\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi}
now follows from the definition (
56
).
∎

In view of Lemma
4.12
, we may (and do) hereafter replace
(L,H)
by
(L^{\mathfrak{K}},H^{K})
and therefore assume that
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=G^{\prime}
and that
\chi
is a faithful character of
G^{\prime}
. In particular,
G^{\prime}
is cyclic and

\Gamma=\ker(\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow G)

is a
p
-group.

5.
Group ring valued Hilbert modular forms

Let
m
be a positive integer. Let
\chi
be an odd character of
G^{\prime}
.
In this section, we use the theory of group ring valued Hilbert modular forms to produce an
R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}
-module

\tilde{B}_{p}
and a cohomology class
\kappa\in H^{1}(G_{F},\tilde{B}_{p})
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
4.5
.
At some point, we will have to assume that
\mathfrak{p}
is not the only prime of
F
above
p
(the case of one prime above
p
will be handled in
§
6
).
We will calculate that

\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}}(\tilde{B}_{p})\subset(p^{m}),

which in conjunction with the
R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}
-module surjection

\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi}\longrightarrow\tilde{B}_{p}

of Theorem
4.5
yields

\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi})\subset(p^{m}).

Since this holds for all
m
, we have
\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}},\chi}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},\chi})=0
. Lemma
4.11
implies
\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}})=0
, which by
Theorem
4.6
completes the proof of the
p
-part of Gross’s Conjecture.

We refer the reader to
[
dk
]
*Section 7 for our definitions on group ring valued Hilbert modular forms, recalling only the essential notation here. Let
\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathcal{O}_{F}
denote an integral ideal and
k\geq 1
a positive integer. We let
M_{k}(\mathfrak{n})
denote the space of Hilbert modular forms of level
\mathfrak{n}
and weight
k
. The subgroup of forms whose
q
-expansion coefficients at all unramified cusps lie in
\mathbf{Z}
is denoted
M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\mathbf{Z})
. If
A
is any abelian group, we let
M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},A)=M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\mathbf{Z})\otimes A
. The space
M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\mathbf{Z})
is endowed with an action of “diamond operators”
S(\mathfrak{m})
, indexed by the classes
\mathfrak{m}\in G_{\mathfrak{n}}^{+}
, the narrow ray class group of
F
associated to the conductor
\mathfrak{n}
.
Suppose now that
R
is a ring and
\boldsymbol{\psi}\colon G_{\mathfrak{n}}^{+}\longrightarrow R^{*}
is a character. Suppose that the abelian group
A
has an
R
-module structure. Then we define

M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},A,\boldsymbol{\psi})=\{f\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},A)\colon f_{|S(\mathfrak{m})}=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{m})f\text{ for all }\mathfrak{m}\in G_{\mathfrak{n}}^{+}\}.

These are the forms of nebentypus
\boldsymbol{\psi}
. In our applications below,
R
is a group ring (or a factor of a group ring), and
\boldsymbol{\psi}
is the tautological character. For this reason, we call
M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},A,\boldsymbol{\psi})
the space of
group ring valued modular forms
. We write
S_{k}(\mathfrak{n},A,\boldsymbol{\psi})\subset M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},A,\boldsymbol{\psi})
for the subspace of cusp forms.

5.1.
The modified group ring Eisenstein series

We begin by recalling the reductions of previous sections. By the results of §
3.2
, we may assume that
T
(and hence
\Sigma^{\prime}
) contains no primes above
p
. By the results of §
4.5
we may assume that

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=G^{\prime}
and that
\chi
is a faithful odd character of
G^{\prime}
. In particular,
G^{\prime}
is cyclic and
\Gamma=\ker(\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow G)
is a
p
-group.

Let

(72)

\mathfrak{n}_{0}=\cond(L/F),\qquad\mathfrak{n}=\lcm(\mathfrak{n}_{0},\ \prod_{\mathclap{\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}}\mathfrak{q}\,).

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be the
p
-part of
\mathfrak{n}
. Note that
\mathfrak{P}\neq 1
as
\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{P}
.
We write

A=R_{\chi}=\mathcal{O}[\mathfrak{g}_{p}]_{\chi},\qquad\overline{A}=\overline{R}_{\chi}=\mathcal{O}[G_{p}]_{\chi}.

There are canonical
\mathcal{O}[\mathfrak{g}]
-algebra injections with finite cokernel:

A\hookrightarrow\prod_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\in\hat{\mathfrak{g}}\\
\psi|_{G^{\prime}}=\chi\end{subarray}}}\mathcal{O}_{\psi},\qquad\overline{A}\hookrightarrow\prod_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi\in\hat{G}\\
\psi|_{G^{\prime}}=\chi\end{subarray}}}\mathcal{O}_{\psi},\qquad x\mapsto(\psi(x))_{\psi}.

Here
\mathcal{O}_{\psi}
denotes the ring
\mathcal{O}
on which
\mathfrak{g}
acts by the character
\psi
. We call the characters indexing these products the
characters of
A
and the
characters of
\overline{A}
,
respectively. In particular, a character of
\overline{A}
is simply a character of
A
that is trivial on
\Gamma
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\psi
be any character of
A
, and let
\mathfrak{c}_{0}=\cond(\psi)
.
Put
\mathfrak{c}=\lcm(\mathfrak{c}_{0},\mathfrak{P})
, and
\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{n}/\mathfrak{c}
. Then
\mathfrak{l}
is a square-free product of primes not dividing
p
.

Proof.

The fact that the primes dividing
\mathfrak{l}
do not lie above
p
is clear since
\mathfrak{P}
is the
p
-part of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{P}\mid\mathfrak{c}
. To prove that
\mathfrak{l}
is square-free, suppose that
\mathfrak{q}
is a prime not lying above
p
such that
\mathfrak{q}^{m}\mid\mid\mathfrak{n}
with
m\geq 2
. It suffices to show that
\mathfrak{q}^{m}\mid\mathfrak{c}
, whence
\mathfrak{q}\nmid\mathfrak{l}
.
By the definition of
\mathfrak{n}
, we must have
\mathfrak{q}^{m}\mid\mathfrak{n}_{0}=\cond(L/F)
.
The proof now follows exactly as
[
dk
]
*Lemma 8.13.
∎

Let
\psi
be a character of
A
. Denote by
\psi_{\mathfrak{P}}
the character
\psi
viewed with modulus divisible by all primes dividing
\mathfrak{P}
. In
[
dk
]
*Definition 8.2, we defined the following linear combination of level-raised Eisenstein series (where
\mathfrak{l}
is as in Lemma
5.1
):

(73)

{W}_{k}(\psi_{\mathfrak{P}},1)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\mid\mathfrak{l}}\mu(\mathfrak{a})\psi(\mathfrak{a})\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{a}^{k}E_{k}(\psi_{\mathfrak{P}},1)|_{\mathfrak{a}}\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\psi).

Here
\mu
is the Möbius function, which for squarefree ideals
\mathfrak{a}
that are the product of
r
distinct primes
satsifies
\mu(\mathfrak{a})=(-1)^{r}
.

As we now recall, we showed in
[
dk
]
*§8 that the forms
W_{k}(\psi_{\mathfrak{P}},1)
interpolate into a group ring-valued family of modular forms, and we calculated the constant terms of this family at certain cusps. Let

\boldsymbol{\psi}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow A^{*},\qquad g=g_{p}g^{\prime}\mapsto g_{p}\chi(g^{\prime})

denote the canonical character. We recall from
[
dk
]
*§7.2.3 our notation on the set of cusps of level
\mathfrak{n}
, denoted
\cusps(\mathfrak{n})
.
A cusp
[\mathcal{A}]
is represented by a pair
\mathcal{A}=(M,\lambda)
where
M\in{\bf GL}_{2}^{+}(F)
, the set of all
2\times 2
matrices over
F
with totally positive determinant, and
\lambda\in\Cl^{+}(F)
, the narrow class group of
F
. The definition of
M_{k}(\mathfrak{n})
involves the choice, for each
\lambda\in\Cl^{+}(F)
, of a representative ideal
\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}
. Writing
\mathfrak{d}
for the different of
F
and letting
M=\begin{pmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{pmatrix}
, we define the ideals

\mathfrak{b}_{\mathcal{A}}=a\mathcal{O}_{F}+c(\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{d})^{-1},\quad\mathfrak{c}_{\mathcal{A}}=c(\mathfrak{t}_{\lambda}\mathfrak{d}\mathfrak{b}_{\mathcal{A}})^{-1}.

Following
[
dk
]
*§7.2.5 we define for any integral ideal
\mathfrak{b}\mid\mathfrak{n}
:

\displaystyle C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{n})

\displaystyle=\{[\mathcal{A}]\in\cusps(\mathfrak{n})\colon\mathfrak{b}\mid\mathfrak{c}_{\mathcal{A}}\}

\displaystyle C_{0}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{n})

\displaystyle=\{[\mathcal{A}]\in\cusps(\mathfrak{n})\colon\gcd(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}_{\mathcal{A}})=1\}.

If
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{n}
, we simply write
C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{n})
and
C_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
. The notion of
normalized constant term
of a modular form at a cusp is defined in
[
dk
]
*§7.2.3.
In the remainder of the paper, we write for simplicity
\Theta_{H}=\Theta_{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{H}
and
\Theta_{L}=\Theta_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{L}
as in §
4
above.

Proposition 5.2
.

There exists a group ring valued form

(74)

W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)\in M_{1}(\mathfrak{n},A,\boldsymbol{\psi})

such that the specialization of
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
at character
\psi
of
A
is the form

W_{1}(\psi_{\mathfrak{P}},1)
.
The normalized constant term of
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
at a cusp
\mathcal{A}
such that
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})

is

(75)

C_{1}^{L}(\mathcal{A})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{N}a)\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(a\mathfrak{b}_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1})\Theta_{L}^{\#}/2^{n}&\text{if }[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{n})\\
0&\text{if }[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})\setminus C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{n}).\end{cases}

Recall here that
\#
denotes the involution on
{R}
induced by
g\mapsto g^{-1}
for
g\in\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof.

For any odd
k\geq 1
,
a group ring valued form

W_{k}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\Frac(A),\boldsymbol{\psi})

is defined in
[
dk
]
*Proposition 8.14, eqn. (102), with notation from
loc. cit.
, by

W_{k}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)=\sum_{\mathfrak{m}\mid\mathfrak{l}}\mathrm{N}I_{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot\tilde{E}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\mathfrak{m}},1)|_{\mathfrak{m}}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\mathfrak{m}}(\mathfrak{m})\frac{1}{\#I_{\mathfrak{m}}}\prod_{v\mid\mathfrak{m}}(1-\mathrm{N}v^{k}).

Note that this definition and the proof that
W_{k}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
specializes under a character
\psi
of
A
to
W_{k}(\psi_{\mathfrak{P}},1)
uses the result of Lemma
5.1
. All the normalized Fourier coefficients of
W_{k}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
lie in
A
, except for possibly the constant terms, which lie in
\Frac(A)
.

As we now explain,
[
dk
]
*Proposition 8.7 implies that the constant term of
W_{1}(\psi_{\mathfrak{P}},1)
at a cusp
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})\setminus C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{n})
vanishes. First note that
\mathfrak{P}\neq 1
and hence
C_{0}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})\cap C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})=\emptyset
.
As in
[
dk
]
*§8.1, write

\mathfrak{c}_{0}=\cond(\psi),\mathfrak{c}=\lcm(\mathfrak{c}_{0},\mathfrak{P}),\text{ and }\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{n}/\mathfrak{c}.

By Lemma
5.1
,
\mathfrak{t}
is a squarefree product of primes not lying above
p
.
We therefore have

C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})\cap C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{c}_{0}\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{n})=C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{n}).

Hence if
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})\setminus C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{n})
, then

[
dk
]
*Prop 8.7 yields that the constant term of
W_{1}(\psi_{\mathfrak{P}},1)
at
[\mathcal{A}]
vanishes in both the cases
\mathfrak{c}_{0}=1
and
\mathfrak{c}_{0}\neq 1
.

At a cusp
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{n})
the contant term of
W_{1}(\psi_{\mathfrak{P}},1)
equals

\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{N}a)\psi^{-1}(a\mathfrak{b}_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1})\frac{L_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\psi,0)}{2^{n}}.

The element of
A
interpolating these specializations is the element
C^{L}_{1}(\mathcal{A})
defined in (
75
). Since
C_{1}^{L}(\mathcal{A})\in A
, we obtain
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)\in M_{1}(\mathfrak{n},A,\boldsymbol{\psi})
.
∎

We have an analogous construction of group ring forms over
G
. We write

\overline{A}=\overline{R}_{\chi}=\mathcal{O}[G_{p}]_{\chi}.

Let

\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\overline{A}^{*}

denote the reduction of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
. Note that
\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
factors through
\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow G
.
As in (
72
), we let

\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\cond(H/F),\qquad\mathfrak{m}=\lcm(\mathfrak{m}_{0},\ \prod_{\mathclap{\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma\cup\Sigma^{\prime}}}\mathfrak{q}\,).

Note that here,
\mathfrak{p}
does not divide the level
\mathfrak{m}
. Since we have
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=G^{\prime}
, however, note that the levels
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{m}
agree away from
p
, i.e. if
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
denotes the
p
-part of
\mathfrak{m}
, then
\mathfrak{n}/\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{P}_{0}
.
(This fact implies that the results of Propositions
5.3
and
5.4
, which
a priori
would apply to the cusps in
C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P}_{0},\mathfrak{m})
, also apply on
C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})
.)

We use
[
dk
]
*Proposition 8.14, eqn. (102) again to define, for odd
k\geq 1
, a group ring valued modular form

(76)

W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\Frac(\overline{A}),\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}})\subset M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\Frac(\overline{A}),\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}).

Again, the
q
-expansion coefficients of
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
other than possibly the constant terms lie in
\overline{A}
.
The specialization of
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
at a character
\psi
of
\overline{A}
is
{W}_{k}(\psi_{\mathfrak{P}_{0}},1)
, defined as in (
73
) with
\mathfrak{P}
replaced by
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
.

We now discuss the constant terms of
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
for odd
k\geq 1
.
The following result follows directly from
[
dk
]
*Proposition 8.7.

Proposition 5.3
.

If
\Sigma\setminus S_{\infty}
is nonempty, the normalized constant term of
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
at a cusp
\mathcal{A}
such that
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})
is

C^{H}_{1}(\mathcal{A})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{N}a){\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}}^{-1}(a\mathfrak{b}_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1})\Theta_{H}^{\#}/2^{n}&\text{if }[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{n})\\
0&\text{if }[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})\setminus C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{n}).\end{cases}

In particular, if
\Sigma\setminus S_{\infty}
is nonempty we have
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)\in M_{1}(\mathfrak{n},\overline{A},\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}})
.

If
\Sigma=S_{\infty}
(in which case
\mathfrak{P}_{0}=1
) the situation is more complicated for
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
.
Furthermore in this case we require the constant terms of the forms
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
for odd
k\geq 3
.

For each character
\psi
of
\overline{A}
, let
\mathfrak{b}_{0}=\cond(\psi)
,
\mathfrak{b}=\lcm(\mathfrak{b}_{0},\mathfrak{P}_{0})
, and
\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{b}
.
If
[\mathcal{A}]
is a cusp, we follow
[
dk
]
*Definition 8.3 and define two sets of primes:

J_{\mathfrak{l}}=\{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{l}\colon[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{0}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{n})\},\qquad J_{\mathfrak{l}}^{c}=\{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{l}\colon[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{n})\}.

Define
C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
to be the unique element of

\Frac(\overline{A})

such that for each character
\psi
of
\overline{A}
, we have

(77)

\psi(C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}))=\frac{\tau(\psi)}{\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{b}_{0}^{k}}\psi(\mathfrak{c}_{\mathcal{A}})\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{N}(-c))\frac{L_{S_{\infty},\emptyset}(\psi^{-1},1-k)}{2^{n}}\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\in J_{\mathfrak{l}}^{c}}(1-\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{q}^{k})\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\in J_{\mathfrak{l}}}(1-\psi(\mathfrak{q}))

if
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{0}(\mathfrak{b}_{0},\mathfrak{n})
, and

\psi(C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}))=0

if
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})\setminus C_{0}(\mathfrak{b}_{0},\mathfrak{n})
. Here
\tau(\psi)
denotes the Gauss sum defined in
[
dka
]
*Definition 4.1. The following proposition follows directly from
[
dk
]
*Propositions 8.6 and 8.7.

Proposition 5.4
.

Suppose that
\Sigma=S_{\infty}
. The normalized constant term of
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
at a cusp
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})
is equal to
C^{H}_{1}(\mathcal{A})+C_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
if
k=1
, and is equal to
C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
if
k>1
.

5.2.
Construction of a cusp form

In this section we construct the cusp form required in our proof. The construction will need to be split into several cases.
Recall that
\mathfrak{P}
is the
p
-part of
\mathfrak{n}
. The ideal
\mathfrak{P}
is divisible precisely by the primes in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=\Sigma\cup\{\mathfrak{p}\}
.
Define
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
to be the product of all other primes above
p
, i.e.

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}=\prod_{\mathclap{\mathfrak{q}\mid p,\ \mathfrak{q}\nmid\mathfrak{P}}}\mathfrak{q}.

•

Case 1: the set
\Sigma\setminus S_{\infty}
is non-empty, i.e.
\mathfrak{p}
is not the only prime dividing
\mathfrak{P}
.

In Case 2, we have
\Sigma=S_{\infty}
.
Note that the eigenvalues of the form
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
for the operator
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
, are
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q})
and
1
. If
\chi(\mathfrak{q})\neq 1
, then these are not congruent modulo the maximal ideal of
A
. To ensure that the Hecke algebras we work with are local, it will be convenient to project to the eigenspace with eigenvalue
1
.
Let

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{0}=\prod_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\\
\chi(\mathfrak{q})\neq 1\end{subarray}}}\mathfrak{q},\qquad\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{1}=\prod_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\\
\chi(\mathfrak{q})=1\end{subarray}}}\mathfrak{q}.

We subdivide Case 2 into three cases:

•

Case 2(a):
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{0}\neq 1
.

•

Case 2(b):
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{0}=1,\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{1}\neq 1
.

•

Case 2(c):
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{1}=1
, i.e.
\mathfrak{p}
is the only prime above
p
in
F
.

We consider the module

\Lambda=A\oplus\overline{A},

endowed with the canonical diagonal
A
-action.
We view
A=A\oplus 0
as a submodule of
\Lambda
and denote the vector
(0,1)\in\Lambda
by
\lambda
, so elements of
\Lambda
will be written
a+\lambda b
with
a\in A,b\in\overline{A}.

As in the previous section, we have the canonical characters

\boldsymbol{\psi}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow A^{*},\qquad\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow\overline{A}^{*}.

We denote the image of
I
in
A
(i.e. the kernel of the canonical projection
A\longrightarrow\overline{A}
) by
I_{A}
.

Recall the following result of Silliman
[
silliman
]
*Theorem 8.10, a generalization of a result of Hida.

Theorem 5.5
.

Fix a positive integer
m
. For positive integers
k\equiv 0\pmod{(p-1)p^{N}}
with
N
sufficiently large depending on
m
,
there is a modular form
V_{k}\in M_{k}(1,\mathbf{Z}_{p},1)
such that

c(\mathfrak{m},V_{k})\equiv 0\pmod{p^{m}}
for all integral ideals
\mathfrak{m}
, and such that the normalized constant term
c_{\mathcal{A}}(0,V_{k})

for each cusp
[\mathcal{A}]
is congruent to
1\pmod{p^{m}}
.

To apply this result, we hereafter assume that
m
is fixed and
k\equiv 1\pmod{(p-1)p^{N})}
with
N
sufficiently large that the conclusion of Theorem
5.5
holds. In subsequent arguments we will make
N
larger still if necessary to obtain other properties of our modular forms.

In Case 1, we define

f=(W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)-\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1))V_{k-1}\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\Lambda,\boldsymbol{\psi}).

Note here that
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},A,\boldsymbol{\psi})
and
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\overline{A},\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}})
by
Propositions
5.2
and
5.3
, respectively.

In Case 2 we
recall the non-zerodivisor

(78)

x=x(k)=\frac{\Theta^{H/F}_{S_{\infty},\emptyset}(1-k)}{\Theta^{H/F}_{S_{\infty},\emptyset}}\in\Frac(\overline{A})

considered in
[
dk
]
*Theorem 8.16.
The elements
\Theta^{H/F}_{S_{\infty},\emptyset}(1-k)
and
\Theta^{H/F}_{S_{\infty},\emptyset}
belong to
\Frac(\overline{A})
and interpolate the nonzero algebraic numbers

L_{S_{\infty},\emptyset}(\psi^{-1},1-k)
and
L_{S_{\infty},\emptyset}(\psi^{-1},0)
, respectively, as
\psi
ranges over the odd characters of
G
that restrict to
\chi
on
G^{\prime}
. For
k\equiv 1\pmod{(p-1)p^{N})}
with
N
sufficiently large, the ratio
x
belongs to
\overline{A}
by
loc. cit.
We hereafter assume that
N
is chosen so that this holds.
Let

(79)

\tilde{x}=\text{ any non-zerodivisor lift of }x\text{ to }A.

We define in Case 2:

(80)

f=\tilde{x}\left(W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)-\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)\right)V_{k-1}+\lambda W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1).

A priori
, in Case 2 the form
f
lies in
M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Q}_{p},\boldsymbol{\psi})
since we have only shown that the constant terms of the forms
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
and
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
lie in
\Frac(\overline{A})=\overline{A}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Q}_{p}
. However, the proposition below shows that in fact
f\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\Lambda,\boldsymbol{\psi})
.
In order to state results in all cases simultaneously, we set
\tilde{x}=1
in Case 1 for the remainder of the paper.

Proposition 5.6
.

With
N
and
k
as above, we have in all cases
f\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\Lambda,\boldsymbol{\psi})
. Furthermore the form
f
has constant terms at cusps
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})

lying in the submodule
J\subset\Lambda
, where

(81)

J=\tilde{x}J_{0},\qquad J_{0}=\left(I_{A}^{2},\Theta_{L}^{\#}-\Theta_{H}^{\#}\lambda,\ p^{m}\Lambda\right).

Proof.

We give the proof in Case 2, as Case 1 is similar and in fact easier.

We first note that the non-constant term
q
-expansion coefficients of
f
lie in
\Lambda
.
Indeed, all the forms appearing in the definition of
f
have non-constant term
q
-expansion coefficients lying in
\Lambda
,
so any failure of integrality of non-constant terms can arise only from multiplying the constant terms of
W_{1}(1,\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}})
by the non-constant terms of
V_{k-1}
. For
k\equiv 1\pmod{(p-1)p^{N}}
and
N
sufficiently large, the non-constant terms of
V_{k-1}
are divisible by any desired power of
p
, and this product will be integral.

We will prove the integrality of the constant terms of
f
and the statement about cuspidality simultaneously.
By Propositions
5.2
and
5.4
, the constant term of
f
at a cusp
[\mathcal{A}]\in C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})
is equal to

\displaystyle\left(C^{L}_{1}(\mathcal{A})-\lambda C^{H}_{1}(\mathcal{A})-\lambda C_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})\right)\tilde{x}y+\lambda C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\equiv\

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{x}C_{1}^{L}(\mathcal{A})-\tilde{x}\lambda C_{1}^{H}(\mathcal{A})\right)-\lambda\left(C_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})\tilde{x}y-C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})\right),

where
y=c_{\mathcal{A}}(0,V_{k-1})\equiv 1\pmod{p^{m}}
and the congruence is modulo
\tilde{x}p^{m}\Lambda\subset J
. For the first term, we have

\left(\tilde{x}C_{1}^{L}(\mathcal{A})-\tilde{x}\lambda C_{1}^{H}(\mathcal{A})\right)=\frac{\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{N}a)\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(a\mathfrak{b}_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1})}{2^{n}}\left(\tilde{x}\Theta_{L}^{\#}-\tilde{x}\Theta_{H}^{\#}\lambda\right)\in J.

It remains to show that
xyC_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})-C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
lies in
\overline{A}
and is divisible by
xp^{m}
. Now

C_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
is some fixed element of
\Frac(\overline{A})
and
x\in\overline{A}
, so for
y
sufficiently close to 1
p
-adically,
(y-1)C_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
will lie in
p^{m}\overline{A}
. Therefore it suffices to show that
xC_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})-C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
lies in
\overline{A}
and is divisible by
xp^{m}
, i.e. that
C_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})-x^{-1}C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
lies in
\overline{A}
and is divisible by
p^{m}
.

For this, we note that by the definition (
78
), multiplying by the factor
x^{-1}
exactly replaces the
L
-value in the definition (
77
) of
C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
with that appearing in
C_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
, hence

C_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})-x^{-1}\Theta_{H}C_{k}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})=C_{1}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})\left(1-\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{b}_{0}^{1-k}\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\in J_{\mathfrak{l}}^{c}}\frac{1-\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{l}_{i}^{k}}{1-\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{l}_{i}}\right).

For
k\equiv 1\pmod{(p-1)p^{N}}
and
N
sufficiently large, the term in parentheses can be made divisible by arbitrarily large powers of
p
. (Note we are in Case 2, whence
\mathfrak{b}_{0}
is prime to
p
.)
The result follows.
∎

Corollary 5.7
.

There exists a
p
-ordinary cusp form
g\in S_{k}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime},\Lambda,\boldsymbol{\psi})^{p\text{-ord}}
such that in Cases 1 and 2(a) we have

g\equiv W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)-\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)\pmod{J_{0}},

while in Cases 2(b) and 2(c) we have

g\equiv\tilde{x}\big(W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)-\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)\big)+\lambda W_{k}(1,\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{p})\pmod{J}.

These congruences are understood to mean that the
q
-expansion coefficients
c(\mathfrak{a},g)
for nonzero ideals
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{F}
are congruent modulo
J_{0}
or
J
to the coefficients of the expressions on the right.

Proof.

Silliman’s result
[
silliman
]
*Theorem 8.4
implies that there is an element
f^{\prime}\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},J,\boldsymbol{\psi})
whose constant terms at
C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})
agree with those of
f
. Therefore
f-f^{\prime}
has constant terms that vanish on
C_{\infty}(\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{n})
.
Let
e_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\ord},e_{p}^{\ord}
denote the ordinary operators of Hida (see
[
dk
]
*§7.2.9).
By

[
dka
]
*Theorem 5.1, the form
g_{0}=e_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\ord}(f-f^{\prime})
is cuspidal,
whence

g=e_{p}^{\ord}(f-f^{\prime})=e_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}^{\ord}g_{0}

is cuspidal as well. Now the ordinary operator
e_{p}^{\ord}
fixes the forms
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
and
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
, whereas it sends
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
to its ordinary
p
-stabilization
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1_{p})
. Furthermore,
V_{k-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p^{m}}
and
p^{m}\in J_{0}
. The result follows in Cases 2(b) and 2(c).

To complete the proof in Cases 1 and 2(a), we apply the operator
\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{0}}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}))
. This operator sends

W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)\mapsto W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{0}},1),\qquad W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)\mapsto W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{0}},1),

while it annihilates
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1_{p}).
This gives the result in Case 1. In Case 2(a), we obtain a cusp form
g
satisfying

g\equiv\tilde{x}\left(W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{0}},1)-\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)\right)\pmod{\tilde{x}J_{0}}.

This congruence in particular implies that the Fourier coefficients of
g
are divisible by
\tilde{x}
. Since
\tilde{x}
is a non-zerodivisor, we may divide by it and obtain a cusp form satsifying the same congruence as in Case 1.
∎

5.3.
Homomorphism from the Hecke algebra

We now define certain Hecke algebras acting on
S_{k}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime},A,\boldsymbol{\psi})
.
We define
\mathbf{T}
to be the
A
-subalgebra of
\End_{A}(S_{k}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime},A,\boldsymbol{\psi}))
generated over
A
by the following operators:

•

T_{\mathfrak{l}}
for
\mathfrak{l}\nmid\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
,

•

S(\mathfrak{a})
for
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})=1
,

•

U_{\mathfrak{q}}
for prime
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma\setminus S_{\infty}
(i.e. for prime
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}
,
\mathfrak{q}\neq\mathfrak{p}
),

•

(U_{\mathfrak{p}}-1)^{2}
, and

•

(U_{\mathfrak{p}}-1)t
for
t\in I
.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}=\mathbf{T}[U_{\mathfrak{p}}]
.
Finally let
\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}=\tilde{\mathbf{T}}[U_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}]
.

Due to the presence of the involution
\#
in Proposition
5.6
, we consider an “involuted” version of the ring
A_{\mathscr{L}}
. Define

A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}=A[{\mathscr{L}}]/({\mathscr{L}}(\Theta_{H})^{\#}-(\Theta_{L})^{\#},{\mathscr{L}}I_{A},{\mathscr{L}}^{2},I_{A}^{2}).

At this point, we must momentarily abandon case 2(c), when
\mathfrak{p}
is the only prime of
F
above
p
. In this case, the last bulleted point of the theorem below—which in some sense is the most important—does not hold. Here
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}=1
, and this last bulleted point then reads
\Ann_{A_{\mathscr{L}}^{\#}}(W)\subset(p^{m})
, which does not hold in case 2(c). The algebra
W
constructed in Theorem
5.8
using the form
g
is not large enough for our purposes. In §
6
we will therefore provide a different approach to proving the congruence (
20
) that only applies when there is exactly one prime of
F
above
p
.
For the remainder of §
5
we assume that there is at least one prime above
p
in
F
other than
\mathfrak{p}
.

Theorem 5.8
.

Suppose we are in Cases 1, 2(a), or 2(b), i.e. there exists a prime of
F
above
p
other than
\mathfrak{p}
. There exists an
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
-algebra
W

and a surjective
A
-algebra homomorphism

\varphi\colon\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}\longrightarrow W

such that:

•

T_{\mathfrak{l}}\mapsto\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{l}^{k-1}+\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{l})
for prime
\mathfrak{l}\nmid\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.

•

S(\mathfrak{a})\mapsto\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{a})
for
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})=1
.

•

U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mapsto 1
for prime
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma\setminus S_{\infty}
.

•

U_{\mathfrak{p}}\mapsto 1-{\mathscr{L}}
.

•

U_{\mathfrak{q}}\mapsto 1
for prime
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}
.

For prime
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
, write
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}=\varphi(U_{\mathfrak{q}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}))\in W
. Then:

•

\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}(\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}-1+\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}))=0
.

•

\Ann_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}}\Big(\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}\Big)\subset(p^{m})
.

Proof.

We recall from (
81
) the definition

J_{0}=\left(I_{A}^{2},\Theta_{L}^{\#}-\Theta_{H}^{\#}\lambda,\ p^{m}\Lambda\right).

To streamline the notation for all cases, write

X=\begin{cases}1&\text{Cases 1/2(a)}\\
\tilde{x}&\text{Case 2(b),}\end{cases}\qquad K=XJ_{0},

and recall
\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}=1
in Case 2(b).
Define

\mathcal{C}=\prod_{\mathfrak{a}\subset O_{F}}\Lambda/{K},

with the product indexed by the nonzero ideals
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{F}
. There is an
A
-module map

c:S_{k}(\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime},\Lambda,\boldsymbol{\psi})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

that associates to each cusp form
h
its collection of normalized
q
-expansion coefficients
c(\mathfrak{a},h)
reduced modulo
{K}
. Note that if we let the operators
T_{\mathfrak{l}}
,
U_{\mathfrak{l}}
act by the usual formulae on Fourier coefficients (see
[
dk
]
*eqn. (97)) and we let
S(\mathfrak{a})
act by
\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{a})
, then the map
c
is equivariant for these operators.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
denote the image of the
\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}
-span of the cusp form
g
defined in Corollary
5.7
under the map
c
. This is an
A
-module of finite type. Define
W
be the image of the canonical
A
-algebra homomorphism

\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}\longrightarrow\End_{A}({\mathcal{F}}).

This construction yields a canonical surjective
A
-algebra map

\varphi:\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}\longrightarrow W

that sends a Hecke operator to its action on the Hecke span of
g
under the map
c
.

We will show in a moment that
W
has the structure of an
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
-algebra. First we calculate the action of Hecke operators on
g
using the congruence of Corollary
5.7
:

(82)

g\equiv\begin{cases}W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)-\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)&\text{Cases 1/2(a)}\\
X\left(W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)-\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)\right)+\lambda W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1_{p})&\text{Case 2(b)}\end{cases}\pmod{K}.

The fact that
\varphi(S(\mathfrak{a}))=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{a})
is clear since all our forms have nebentypus
\boldsymbol{\psi}
.
The operator
T_{\mathfrak{l}}
acts by multiplication by
1+\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{l})
on
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
and
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
, and it acts by multiplication by
\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{l}^{k-1}+\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{l})
on
W_{k-1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1_{p})
. Since
\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{l}^{k-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p^{m}}
and
Xp^{m}\in K
, we have

T_{\mathfrak{l}}(g)\equiv(\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{l}^{k-1}+\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{l}))g\pmod{K}.

Similarly
\varphi(U_{\mathfrak{q}})=1
for prime
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma\setminus S_{\infty}
, since all the forms
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
,
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1),W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1_{p})
have
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
-eigenvalue 1.

The most interesting action is that of
U_{\mathfrak{p}}
. This operator fixes
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
, since
\mathfrak{p}\mid\mathfrak{P}
. It also acts as
\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(\mathfrak{p})=1
on
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1_{p})
because of the
p
-stabilization. Yet
U_{\mathfrak{p}}
does not act as a scalar on
