W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
, as it is not stabilized. Instead
U_{\mathfrak{p}}-1
sends
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
to its
\mathfrak{p}
-stabilization
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1_{\mathfrak{p}})=W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{p}},1)
, where the equality follows since
\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(\mathfrak{p})=1
and we are in weight 1. The conclusion of these considerations is that

(83)

(U_{\mathfrak{p}}-1)g\equiv-\lambda XW_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)\pmod{K}.

We can now give
W
the structure of an
A_{\mathscr{L}}^{\#}
-algebra by letting
{\mathscr{L}}
act by
\varphi(1-U_{\mathfrak{p}})
. To prove that this is well-defined, we must show that the relations in
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
are satisfied in
W
, namely
{\mathscr{L}}(\Theta^{H})^{\#}-(\Theta^{L})^{\#}={\mathscr{L}}I_{A}={\mathscr{L}}^{2}=I_{A}^{2}=0
. The most interesting of these is the first. From (
83
), we find the following congruences mod

K
:

\displaystyle(\Theta^{H})^{\#}(1-U_{\mathfrak{p}})g

\displaystyle\equiv(\Theta^{H})^{\#}X\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)

\displaystyle\equiv(\Theta^{L})^{\#}XW_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)

\displaystyle\equiv(\Theta^{L})^{\#}g.

The second congruence follows from the definition of
K
and the third from (
82
), in view of the fact that
(\Theta_{L})^{\#}\in I_{A}

annihilates
\lambda
while
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)\equiv W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)\pmod{I_{A}}
and

X(\Theta^{L})^{\#}I_{A}\in XI_{A}^{2}\subset K
. This proves that the first relation holds, while the others are similar but easier.

Next we study the action of
U_{\mathfrak{q}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q})
for
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
. This operator sends
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1)
to
W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\mathfrak{q}},1)
, sends
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1)
to
W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{q}},1)
, and annihilates

W_{k}(\boldsymbol{\psi},1_{p})
. For
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}_{1}^{\prime}
, we therefore have

(84)

(U_{\mathfrak{q}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}))g\equiv X\left(W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)-\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{q}\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}},1)\right)\pmod{K}.

Note that
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
acts as 1 on the form on the right. This yields the equation

(85)

\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}(\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}-1+\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}))=0,

where
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}=\varphi(U_{\mathfrak{q}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}))
. Meanwhile if
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}
, then in particular we are in case 1/2(a) and
U_{\mathfrak{q}}
acts as 1 on
g\pmod{K}
by (
82
). For such
\mathfrak{q}
we have

(86)

\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}=1-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q})

and (
85
) holds trivially.

To prove the last item note that by (
84
) and (
86
) we have

(87)

\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}(U_{\mathfrak{q}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}))g\equiv X\left(W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}},1)-\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}},1)\right)\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}}(1-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}))\pmod{K}.

In case 2(c), this congruence does not hold as there is a contribution from the term
W_{k}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}},1_{p})
. This term does not appear in (
82
) in cases 1/2(a), and is annihilated by the single application of an
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}
for
\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{1}
in case 2(b). This is why case 2(c) must be removed from the present analysis.

Returning to (
87
), suppose this form is annihilated by an element
a+b{\mathscr{L}}\in A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
, with
a,b\in A
. By definition, such an element acts by
a+b(1-U_{\mathfrak{p}})
and hence, noting that
\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}_{0}^{\prime}}(1-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}))
is a unit, we obtain

aX\left(W_{1}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}},1)-\lambda W(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}},1)\right)+bX\lambda W_{1}(\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}},1_{\mathfrak{p}})\equiv 0\pmod{K}.

Analyzing the
q
-expansion coefficients
c(1,*)
and
c(\mathfrak{p},*)
of this congruence, respectively, we obtain

\displaystyle X(a-\lambda a+\lambda b)

\displaystyle\equiv 0\pmod{K}

\displaystyle X(a-2\lambda a+\lambda b)

\displaystyle\equiv 0\pmod{K}.

From these, we deduce
X(a+\lambda b)\in K
, and since
X
is a non-zerodivisor it follows that
a+\lambda b\in J_{0}=(\Theta_{H}^{\#}\lambda-\Theta_{L}^{\#},I_{A}^{2}\Lambda,p^{m}\Lambda)
. It follows that in
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
, we have

a+b{\mathscr{L}}\in(\Theta_{H}^{\#}{\mathscr{L}}-\Theta_{L}^{\#},I_{A}^{2},p^{m})=(p^{m})

as desired.
∎

5.4.
Galois Representation

In this section we recall the formalism of
[
dk
]
*§9.1–9.2 regarding the Galois representation associated to the Hecke algebra
\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}
.
As explained in
[
dk
]
*§8.5 the Hecke algebra
{\mathbf{T}}^{\dagger}
is reduced.
The kernel of
\varphi
is contained in a unique maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}
. This maximal ideal is generated by the maximal ideal
(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathcal{O}},I)
of
A
together with the elements
T_{\mathfrak{l}}-(1+\chi(\mathfrak{l}))
for
\mathfrak{l}\nmid\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
,
S(\mathfrak{a})-\chi(\mathfrak{a})
for
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{n}\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})=1
,
U_{\mathfrak{q}}-1
for all
\mathfrak{q}\mid p
.

Let
\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}
denote the completion of
\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}
with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
(and similarly
\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
the completions of
\mathbf{T}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
with respect to their maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}\cap\mathbf{T}
,
\mathfrak{m}\cap\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
, respectively). Set

K=\Frac(\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}).

As in
[
dk
]
*§9.2, there is a Galois representation

\rho:G_{F}\longrightarrow{\bf GL}_{2}(K)

such that

(1)

\rho
is unramified outside
\mathfrak{n}p
.

(2)

For all primes
\mathfrak{l}\nmid\mathfrak{n}p
, the characteristic polynomial of
\rho(\Frob_{\mathfrak{l}})
is given by

(88)

{\rm char}(\rho(\Frob_{\mathfrak{l}}))(x)=x^{2}-T_{\mathfrak{l}}x+\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{l})\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{l}^{k-1}.

(3)

For
\mathfrak{q}\mid p
, let
G_{F,\mathfrak{q}}\subset G_{F}
denote a decomposition group at
\mathfrak{q}
. We have

(89)

\rho|_{G_{F,\mathfrak{q}}}\sim\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\psi}\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{k-1}\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}&*\\
0&\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}\end{pmatrix},

where
\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}\colon G_{F,\mathfrak{q}}\longrightarrow(\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger})^{*}
is the unramified character given by
\eta_{\mathfrak{p}}(\rec(\varpi^{-1}))=U_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Here
\varpi
denotes a uniformizer of
F_{\mathfrak{q}}^{*}
and

\rec\colon F_{\mathfrak{q}}^{*}\longrightarrow G_{F,\mathfrak{q}}^{\text{ab}}

is the local Artin reciprocity map.

For each
\mathfrak{q}\mid p
, let
V_{\mathfrak{q}}
be the eigenspace of
\rho|_{G_{\mathfrak{q}}}
i.e. the span of the vector
\binom{1}{0}
in the basis for which (
89
) holds.
We choose an element
\tau\in G_{F}
as in
[
dk
]
*Proposition 9.3 so that its restriction to
\mathfrak{g}
is complex conjugation and so that for all
\mathfrak{q}\mid p
, the subspace
V_{\mathfrak{q}}
projected to each factor of
K
is not stable under
\rho(\tau)
. As in
loc. cit.,
fix a basis such that

(90)

\rho(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}

where
\lambda_{1}\equiv 1\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\lambda_{2}\equiv-1\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}
. For a general
\sigma
, we write

\rho(\sigma)=\begin{pmatrix}a(\sigma)&b(\sigma)\\
c(\sigma)&d(\sigma)\end{pmatrix}.

For each
\mathfrak{q}\mid p
, there is a change of basis matrix
M_{\mathfrak{q}}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\mathfrak{q}}&B_{\mathfrak{q}}\\
C_{\mathfrak{q}}&D_{\mathfrak{q}}\end{pmatrix}\in{\bf GL}_{2}(K)
such that

(91)

\begin{pmatrix}a(\sigma)&b(\sigma)\\
c(\sigma)&d(\sigma)\end{pmatrix}M_{\mathfrak{q}}=M_{\mathfrak{q}}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\psi}\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{k-1}\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}&*\\
0&\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}\end{pmatrix}.

The choice of
\tau
ensures that
A_{\mathfrak{q}}
and
C_{\mathfrak{q}}
are invertible in
K
. Furthermore, equating the upper left hand entries in (
91
) yields:

(92)

b(\sigma)=x_{\mathfrak{q}}(a(\sigma)-\boldsymbol{\psi}\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{k-1}\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}^{-1}(\sigma))\text{ for all }\sigma\in G_{F,\mathfrak{q}},\text{ where }x_{\mathfrak{q}}=-\frac{A_{\mathfrak{q}}}{C_{\mathfrak{q}}}.

5.5.
Cohomology Class

Let
\varphi_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger}\longrightarrow W
denote the extension of the homomorphism
\varphi
to the completion of
\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}
.
Let

\mathbf{I}^{\dagger}=\ker(\varphi_{\mathfrak{m}}),\quad\tilde{\mathbf{I}}=\ker({\varphi}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}),\quad\mathbf{I}=\ker(\varphi_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}}).

As in
[
dk
]
*§9.3, the choice of basis for
\rho
implies that
a(\sigma),d(\sigma)\in\mathbf{T}
with

(93)

a(\sigma)\equiv\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{k-1}(\sigma)\pmod{\mathbf{I}},\qquad d(\sigma)\equiv\boldsymbol{\psi}(\sigma)\pmod{\mathbf{I}}.

Furthermore we have

(94)

\det\rho(\sigma)\equiv\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{k-1}\boldsymbol{\psi}(\sigma).

Recall that
K=\Frac(\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\dagger})
.
Recall the elements
x_{\mathfrak{q}}\in K
for
\mathfrak{q}\mid p
defined in (
92
), including the distinguished prime
\mathfrak{p}
.
Define
x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}=x_{\mathfrak{p}}(U_{\mathfrak{p}}-1)
. Let:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{B}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}\text{-submodule of }K\text{ generated by }b(\sigma)\text{ for all }\sigma\in G_{F}\text{ along with }

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ x_{\mathfrak{p}},x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime},\text{ and }x_{\mathfrak{q}}\text{ for all }\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma.

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}\text{-submodule of }\tilde{\mathbf{B}}\text{ generated by }\tilde{\mathbf{I}}\tilde{\mathbf{B}},p^{m}\tilde{\mathbf{B}},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ b(\sigma)\text{ for all }\sigma\in I_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}.

(95)

\displaystyle\tilde{B}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{B}}/\tilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}.

By construction,
\tilde{B}
naturally has the structure of an
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
-algebra in which
{\mathscr{L}}
acts by multiplication by
(1-U_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

Denote by
\overline{b}(\sigma)
the image of
b(\sigma)
in
\tilde{B}
.
Since
\rho
is a Galois representation, we have

b(\sigma\sigma^{\prime})=a(\sigma)b(\sigma^{\prime})+b(\sigma)d(\sigma^{\prime})\qquad\text{ for all }\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in G_{F}.

The congruences (
93
) therefore imply that the function

\kappa(\sigma)=\overline{b}(\sigma)\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)

is a 1-cocycle defining a cohomology class
[\kappa]\in H^{1}(G_{F},\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}))
. Note here that since
p^{m}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}=0
in
\tilde{B}
and
\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{k-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p^{m}}
, the character
\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{k-1}
acts trivially on
\tilde{B}
.
Let:

\displaystyle{B}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}\text{-submodule of }\tilde{B}\text{ generated by the image of }b(\sigma)\text{ for all }\sigma\in G_{F}.

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}\text{-submodule of }\tilde{B}\text{ generated by }B_{0}\text{ along with }

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ x_{\mathfrak{p}},x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime},\text{ and }x_{\mathfrak{q}}\text{ for all }\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma.

Note that
\tilde{\mathbf{I}}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}=0
in
\tilde{B}
and every element of
\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is equivalent to an element of
A
modulo
\mathbf{I}
(see the first three bullet points of Theorem
5.8
). Therefore, in the definition of
B_{0}
and
B
, it is equivalent to replace
\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}}
by
A
.

The
A
-module structure of
\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})
is by definition the composition of the involution
\#
with the natural
A
-module structure of
\tilde{B}
. The canonical
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
-module structure of
\tilde{B}
can therefore be viewed as an
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}
-module structure on
\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})
.

We now verify that our construction verifies all the properties required to apply Theorems
4.4
and
4.5
.
First we describe the class
\kappa
locally at primes in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}\setminus S_{\infty}
using (
92
).

Lemma 5.9
.

For
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma\setminus S_{\infty}
, we have

\kappa(\sigma)=x_{\mathfrak{q}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)-1),\qquad\sigma\in G_{F,\mathfrak{q}}.

Meanwhile if
x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}=-{\mathscr{L}}x_{\mathfrak{p}}
then

\kappa(\sigma)=x_{\mathfrak{p}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)-1)+\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\sigma)x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime},\qquad\sigma\in G_{F,\mathfrak{p}},

where
\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}
is as in (
39
).

Proof.

Note that

(96)

a(\sigma)\equiv\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{k-1}(\sigma)\equiv 1\pmod{(\mathbf{I},p^{m})}.

If
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma\setminus S_{\infty},
then
U_{\mathfrak{q}}\equiv 1\pmod{\mathbf{I}}
, and hence by the definition of
\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}
following (
89
),

\eta_{\mathfrak{q}}
acts trivially on
\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})
. Therefore (
92
) becomes

\kappa(\sigma)=\overline{b}(\sigma)\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)=x_{\mathfrak{q}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)-1),\qquad\sigma\in G_{F,\mathfrak{q}}.

For
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{p}
, this applies except that
U_{\mathfrak{p}}\not\equiv 1\pmod{\mathbf{I}}
.
Since
(U_{\mathfrak{p}}-1)^{2}\in\mathbf{I}
, we have

U_{\mathfrak{p}}^{n}\equiv 1+n(U_{\mathfrak{p}}-1)\pmod{\mathbf{I}}.

It follows that

\kappa(\sigma)=x_{\mathfrak{p}}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)-1)+\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\sigma)x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime},\qquad\sigma\in G_{F,\mathfrak{p}},

where

x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}=(U_{\mathfrak{p}}-1)x_{\mathfrak{p}}=-{\mathscr{L}}x_{\mathfrak{p}}.\qed

Theorem 5.10
.

The
G
-module
\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})
and the cohomology class
[\kappa]\in H^{1}(G_{F},\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}))
satisfy the following properties.

•

The class
[\kappa]
is unramified outside
\Sigma^{\prime}
, locally trivial at
\Sigma
, and tamely ramified at
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

•

The image of the restriction

[\kappa]_{|G_{L}}\in H^{1}(G_{L},\tilde{B})=\Hom_{\cont}(G_{L},\tilde{B})

is equal to
{B}_{0}
.

•

The quotient

{B}/{B}_{0}
is generated over
A
by
x_{\mathfrak{q}}
for
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma
and the elements
x_{\mathfrak{p}},x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
. The element
x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
is fixed by the action of
G_{F,\mathfrak{p}}
.

•

The 1-cocycle
\kappa
satisfies the following.

–

For
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma
finite and
\sigma\in G_{F,\mathfrak{q}}
, we have

\kappa(\sigma)=(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)-1)x_{\mathfrak{q}}.

–

For
\sigma\in\WD(\overline{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}/F_{\mathfrak{p}})
, we have

(97)

\kappa(\sigma)=(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)-1)x_{\mathfrak{p}}+\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\sigma)x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}.

–

Let
\tau
be the special element used in §
5.4
to fix the chosen basis of
\rho
. For all
\sigma\in G_{F}
, we have

(98)

\kappa(\sigma)=[\kappa]|_{G_{L}}({\sigma}\tau{\sigma}^{-1}\tau^{-1})/2\in B_{0}.

•

With respect to the
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}
-structure on
\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})
, we have

x_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}+{\mathscr{L}}x_{\mathfrak{p}}=0.

Proof.

The Galois representation
\rho
, and hence the cohomology class
[\kappa]
, is unramified outside
\Sigma\cup\Sigma^{\prime}
and the primes dividing
p
. There are three types of primes above
p
: those in
\Sigma
, the distinguished prime
\mathfrak{p}
, and the primes dividing
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.

Since in the definition of
\tilde{B}
we have taken the quotient by the
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
-module spanned by
b(\sigma)
for
\sigma\in I_{\mathfrak{q}},\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
, it follows that
[\kappa]
is unramified at the primes dividing
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.
For
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma\setminus S_{\infty}
, Lemma
5.9

expresses
\kappa_{|G_{F,\mathfrak{q}}}
as a coboundary and demonstrates that
[\kappa]
is locally trivial at
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma
. At
\mathfrak{p}
, note that since
\ord_{\mathfrak{p}}(\sigma)=0
for
\sigma\in I_{\mathfrak{p}}
, Lemma
5.9
shows that
[\kappa]
is unramified at
\mathfrak{p}
. To conclude the proof of the first item, note that
\Sigma^{\prime}
contains no primes above
p
, and all our modules are pro-
p
. Therefore
[\kappa]
is tamely ramified at all primes in
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

For the second bullet point, let
B_{L}\subset B_{0}
denote the image of
[\kappa]_{|G_{L}}
. Then of course the image of
[\kappa]_{|G_{L}}
in
H^{1}(G_{L},(B_{0}/B_{L})(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}))
vanishes, and hence by
[
dk
]
*Lemma 6.3, the image of
[\kappa]
in
H^{1}(G_{F},(B_{0}/B_{L})(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}))
vanishes. Writing
\overline{\kappa}
for the image of
\kappa
in
B_{0}/B_{L}
, we may then write

\overline{\kappa}(\sigma)=z(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)-1)

for some
z\in B_{0}/B_{L}
. Yet by construction
\kappa(\tau)=0
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\tau)=-1
. We therefore obtain
z=0
. Hence
\overline{\kappa}=0
as a cocycle. But by definition of
\kappa
and
B_{0}
, the image of the cocycle
\kappa
generates
B_{0}
and hence the image of
\overline{\kappa}
generates
B_{0}/B_{L}
. It follows that
B_{0}/B_{L}=0
, i.e.
B_{0}=B_{L}
as desired.

Next we show that
\kappa
satisfies equation (
98
). As
B_{0}
is pro-
p
with
p
odd, it is enough to show that

2\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)\overline{b}(\sigma)=\overline{b}(\sigma\tau\sigma^{-1}\tau^{-1}),

where
\tau
is as in (
90
). The upper right entry (“
b
-entry”) of
\rho(\sigma\tau\sigma^{-1}\tau^{-1})
is

(99)

b(\sigma\tau\sigma^{-1}\tau^{-1})=\det(\rho(\sigma))^{-1}a(\sigma)b(\sigma)\left(1+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\right).

The congruence (
94
) yields

\delta\equiv\boldsymbol{\psi}(\sigma)\pmod{p^{m},\mathbf{I}}.

The first congruence in (
93
) yields

a(\sigma)\equiv 1\pmod{p^{m},\mathbf{I}}.

Furthermore,
\lambda_{1}\equiv 1\equiv-\lambda_{2}\pmod{p^{m},\mathbf{I}}
. Hence the expression on the right side of (
99
) is congruent to
2\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}(\sigma)\overline{b}(\sigma)
. This finishes the proof that
\kappa
satisfies equation (
98
).

The remaining bullet points follow directly from the definitions or have already been established.
∎

In view of Theorems
4.4
and
4.5
, we deduce from Theorem
5.10
:

Corollary 5.11
.

We have an
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}
-module surjection

\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},A}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1})

and hence an inclusion

\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},A})\subset\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}))
.

5.6.
Calculation of the Fitting ideal

It remains to prove that

\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}))\subset(p^{m}).

Applying the involution
\#
, this is equivalent to

\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}}(\tilde{B})\subset(p^{m})=p^{m}A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}.

This removal of the twist by
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}
will be convenient so that the usual
\mathfrak{g}
-module structure on
\tilde{B}
via
\boldsymbol{\psi}
is compatible with the
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
-module structure on
\tilde{B}
and the homomorphism
\varphi_{\mathfrak{m}}
, which satsifies
\varphi_{\mathfrak{m}}(S(\mathfrak{a}))=\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{a})
.

We first recall the following lemma from
[
dk
]
*Lemma 9.9.

Lemma 5.12
.

Recall that
\mathbf{B}_{0}\subset\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
denotes the
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
-submodule generated by the elements
b(\sigma)
for
\sigma\in G_{F}
.
There are finitely many elements
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in{\mathbf{B}}_{0}
that are non-zerodivisors in
K
, which generate
\mathbf{B}_{0}
as an
A
-module.

Theorem 5.13
.

With notation as in Theorem
5.8
, we have

\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}}(\tilde{B})\subset\bigg(p^{m},\Ann_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}}\Big(\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}\cdot W\Big)\bigg),

and hence by the last item of that theorem we have

\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}}(\tilde{B})\subset(p^{m}).

Proof.

The proof proceeds closely along the lines of that in
[
dk
]
*Theorem 9.10. Let
\mathfrak{q}_{1},\dotsc,\mathfrak{q}_{r}
denote the primes dividing
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
. For each
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
, choose an element
\sigma_{i}\in G_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}\subset G_{F}
that lifts
\rec(\varpi_{i})\in G_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}^{\text{ab}}
, where

\varpi_{i}
is a uniformizer for
F_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}
. Define

c_{i}:=-b(\sigma_{i})\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}_{i})\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{1-k}(\sigma_{i})=x_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}(U_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}_{i})+\mathbf{I})\in\tilde{\mathbf{B}}.

Here and throughout this proof, we use the notation
a=b+\mathbf{I}
to mean
a=b+z
for some
z\in\mathbf{I}
to avoid needing to add distinct variable names for each such
z
that appears.

By choosing the elements
b_{1},\dots,b_{m}
from Lemma
5.12
together with
x_{\mathfrak{q}}
for all
\mathfrak{q}\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we get elements
b_{1},\dotsc,b_{n}
(
n=m+\#\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
) of
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
that are non-zerodivisors in
K=\Frac(\mathbf{T}_{\mathfrak{m}})
and generate this module over
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
. The images of these elements in
\tilde{B}
are therefore
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
-module generators.

To calculate
\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}}(\tilde{B})
we use the generating set
c_{1},\dotsc,c_{r},b_{1},\dotsc,b_{n}
for
\tilde{B}
over
A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
. Of course, these first
r
generators are not necessary, but including them will aid us in proving the theorem. Suppose we have a matrix

M\in M_{(n+r)\times(n+r)}(A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#})

such that each row of
M
represents a relation amongst our generators, i.e. such that

M(c_{1},\dotsc,c_{r},b_{1},\dotsc,b_{n})^{T}\equiv 0\text{ in }\tilde{B}^{n+r}.

We need to show that
\det(M)\in A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
satisfies
(\det(M)+p^{m}z)\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}=0
in
W
for some
z\in A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}
.

Write
M=(Y|Z)
in block matrix form, where

Y=(y_{ij})\in M_{(n+r)\times r}(A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}),\qquad Z=(z_{ij})\in M_{(n+r)\times n}(A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}).

Since
\boldsymbol{\psi}
and
\eta_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}
are unramified at
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
and
a(\sigma)\equiv\varepsilon_{\cyc}^{k-1}\pmod{\mathbf{I}}
, we have by (
92
):

b(I_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}})\subset x_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}\mathbf{I}.

Also, since the
b_{i}
generate
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
, every element of
\tilde{\mathbf{I}}\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
can be written as a sum of elements of the form
t_{i}b_{i}
with
t_{i}\in\tilde{\mathbf{I}}
.
Therefore each relation

\sum_{j=1}^{r}y_{ij}c_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}z_{ij}b_{j}\equiv 0\text{ in }\tilde{B}

can be expressed as in equality in
\tilde{\mathbf{B}}
as

(100)

\sum_{j=1}^{r}x_{\mathfrak{q}_{j}}(\tilde{y}_{ij}(U_{\mathfrak{q}_{j}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}_{j}))+\tilde{\mathbf{I}})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\tilde{z}_{ij}+\tilde{\mathbf{I}}+p^{m}\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}})b_{j}=0.

We reiterate that here and in what follows, the symbols
\tilde{\mathbf{I}}
(twice) and
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
represent elements of those sets for which we do not, for notational reasons, introduce separate variable names. Here we have denoted by
\tilde{y}_{ij}
and
\tilde{z}_{ij}
elements of
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
such that
\varphi(\tilde{y}_{ij})=y_{ij}
,
\varphi(\tilde{z}_{ij})=z_{ij}
.
It follows from (
100
) that if we define a matrix
M^{\prime}\in M_{(n+r)\times(n+r)}(\Frac(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}}))
in block form by

M^{\prime}=\left(x_{\mathfrak{q}_{j}}(\tilde{y}_{ij}(U_{\mathfrak{q}_{j}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}_{j}))+\tilde{\mathbf{I}})\ \ \ \ |\ \ \ \ (\tilde{z}_{ij}+\tilde{\mathbf{I}}+p^{m}\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}})b_{j}\right),

then
\det(M^{\prime})=0
in
K
since it has rows that sum to 0. We can cancel the factors
x_{\mathfrak{q}_{i}}
and
b_{j}
scaling the columns of
M^{\prime}
, since these are non-zerodivisors in
K
. We obtain that
\det(M^{\prime\prime})=0
where

M^{\prime\prime}=\left((\tilde{y}_{ij}(U_{\mathfrak{q}_{j}}-\boldsymbol{\psi}(\mathfrak{q}_{j}))+\tilde{\mathbf{I}})\ \ |\ \ (\tilde{z}_{ij}+\tilde{\mathbf{I}}+p^{m}\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathfrak{m}})\right)\in M_{(n+r)\times(n+r)}(\mathbf{T}^{\dagger}).

Taking the determinant of
M^{\prime\prime}
and applying
\varphi
, we obtain

0=\varphi(\det(M^{\prime\prime}))=(\det(M)+p^{m}A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#})\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}\text{ in }W.

Therefore,

\det(M)\in\bigg(p^{m},\Ann_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}^{\#}}\Big(\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\epsilon_{\mathfrak{q}}\cdot W\Big)\bigg),

yielding the first statement of the theorem.
The second statement then follows immediately from the last bulleted statement in Theorem
5.8
.
∎

We immediately find:

Theorem 5.14
.

Suppose we are in cases 1, 2(a), or 2(b).
We have

\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},A})=0.

Proof.

Corollary
5.11
and Theorem
5.13
combine to yield

\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},A})\subset\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\tilde{B}(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{-1}))\subset(p^{m}).

Since this holds for all
m
, we have
\Fitt_{A_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}},A})=0
as desired.
∎

Combining our results yields the
p
-part of Gross’s conjecture.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

For now we assume there is more than one prime above
p
in
F
, leaving the case of one prime for the next section.
Theorem
5.14
and Lemma
4.11
imply that

\Fitt_{R_{{\mathscr{L}}}}(\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}})=0.
Theorem
4.6
then yields
the
p
-part of the modified Gross conjecture:

\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})\equiv\Theta_{L}\pmod{I^{2}}.

Lemma
3.1
now gives the
p
-part of Gross’s conjecture for the Brumer–Stark unit
u_{\mathfrak{p}}
:

\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}})\equiv\Theta_{S_{\mathfrak{p}},T}^{L/F}\pmod{I^{2}}.\qed

6.
The case of one prime above
p
in
F

In this section we handle Case 2(c), where
\mathfrak{p}
is the only prime of
F
lying above
p
. We impose this condition for the remainder of the paper.
Rather than calculating the Fitting ideal of
\nabla_{\!{\mathscr{L}}}
, we prove the
p
-part of the modified Gross Conjecture (the congruence (
20
)) by taking advantage of two features that present themselves when there is only one prime above
\mathfrak{p}
: (1) the cyclotomic tower is ramified only at
\mathfrak{p}
, hence we may deform up this tower without altering the depletion set
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
; (2) The rank one rational Gross–Stark conjecture, proven in
[
ddp
]
and
[
v
]
, is known. In essence, our argument is to show that the rational conjecture (for the cyclotomic tower) implies the integral conjecture (for arbitrary
L/F
). The key input in this reduction is the strong version of the Brumer–Stark conjecture giving the Fitting ideal of
\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}
, conjectured by Burns and Kurihara and proven in
[
dk
]
. This result was stated in Theorem
4.2
above, and we apply it in this section to the compositum of
L
with cyclotomic extensions of
F
. Another important result applied is the nonvanishing of the derivative of
p
-adic
L
-functions at
s=0
, which follows by combining the result of the rational Gross–Stark conjecture with the spectacular transcendence theorem of Brumer–Baker on the linear independence of
p
-adic logarithms of algebraic numbers. This again takes advantage of the fact that we are in a rank 1 situation since there is only one prime above
p
in
F
.

We begin by recalling the necessary results and notation concerning
p
-adic
L
-functions.

6.1.
p
-adic
L
-functions

In our current setting we have

\Sigma=S_{\infty}
,
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}=S_{\infty}\cup\{\mathfrak{p}\}
, and
\mathfrak{p}
is the only prime of
F
above
p
.
For each odd character
\chi
of
G
, Deligne–Ribet and Cassou-Nogues construct a
p
-adic meromorphic function

L_{p}(\chi\omega,s)\colon\mathbf{Z}_{p}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\chi)

satisfying the interpolation property

L_{p}(\chi\omega,n)=L_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\emptyset}(\chi\omega^{n},n)

for all integers
n\leq 0
. Here
\omega\colon G_{F}\longrightarrow\mu_{p-1}
denotes the Teichmüller character and
\mathbf{Q}_{p}(\chi)
denotes the extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{p}
obtained by adjoining the values of
\chi
. Analyticity and integrality are achieved if we incorporate the smoothing set
\Sigma^{\prime}
, i.e. we have a
p
-adic analytic function

L_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}(\chi\omega,s)\colon\mathbf{Z}_{p}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}(\chi)

satisfying

L_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}(\chi\omega,n)=L_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\chi\omega^{n},n)

for all integers
n\leq 0
. Moreover, these
p
-adic
L
-functions interpolate to a group ring valued Stickelberger function.
There exists a
p
-adic analytic function

\Theta_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{H}(s)\colon\mathbf{Z}_{p}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}[G]^{-}

satisfying the interpolation property

\Theta_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{H}(1-k)=\Theta_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}(1-k)

for all positive integers
k\equiv 1\pmod{p-1}
. As we now describe,
\Theta_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{H}(s)
can be constructed as a certain
p
-adic integral.
Let
F_{\infty}/F
denote the cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension of
F
and let
\mathfrak{h}_{\infty}={\rm Gal}(HF_{\infty}/F)
.
For each integer
m\geq 0
we let
F_{m}\subset F_{\infty}
denote the
m
th layer of the tower and let
\mathfrak{h}_{m}={\rm Gal}(HF_{\infty}/HF_{m})
.
Then
\mathfrak{h}_{m}
is an open subgroup of
\mathfrak{h}_{\infty}
and its cosets provide a cover of
\mathfrak{h}_{\infty}
by disjoint opens.
For
\sigma\in G_{m}=\mathfrak{h}_{\infty}/\mathfrak{h}_{m}\cong{\rm Gal}(HF_{m}/F)
we define

\mu(\sigma+\mathfrak{h}_{m})=\zeta_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\sigma,0),\quad\text{where }\Theta_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}^{HF_{m}/F}(0)=\sum_{\tau\in G_{m}}\zeta_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\tau,0)\tau^{-1}.

For
\sigma\in G
define

(101)

\zeta_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}(\sigma,s)=\int_{\sigma+\mathfrak{h}_{0}}\langle\varepsilon_{\cyc}(\tau)\rangle^{-s}d\mu(\tau).

Here
\mathfrak{h}_{0}={\rm Gal}(HF_{\infty}/H)
. We then have

\Theta_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{H}(s)=\sum_{\sigma\in G}\zeta_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}(\sigma,s)\sigma^{-1}.

Taking the derivative of (
101
) with respect to
s
and evaluating at 0, we obtain

(102)

\zeta_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\prime}(\sigma,0)=-\int_{\sigma+\mathfrak{h}_{0}}\log_{p}\varepsilon_{\cyc}(\tau)d\mu(\tau).

To evaluate this modulo
p^{m}
, we may take the Riemann sum over the cosets of
\mathfrak{h}_{m}
. We obtain:

Lemma 6.1
.

For every integer
m\geq 0
, we have

(\Theta_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{H})^{\prime}(0)\equiv-\sum_{\sigma\in G_{m}}\zeta_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\sigma,0)\log_{p}\varepsilon_{\cyc}(\sigma)\overline{\sigma}^{-1}\pmod{p^{m}}.

Here
\overline{\sigma}
denotes the image of
\sigma
in
G
.

For notational simplicity, we will simply write
\Theta_{H}^{\prime}
for
(\Theta_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{H})^{\prime}(0)
in the sequel.

6.2.
The rational Gross–Stark conjecture

The following result, which we refer to as the rank 1 rational Gross–Stark conjecture, was proven in
[
ddp
]
and
[
v
]
. The latter paper removed two assumptions from the former, making the result unconditional.

Theorem 6.2

(
[
gross
]
*Conjecture 2.12)
.

Let
u\in U_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-}
such that

(103)

\chi(\ord_{G}(u))=\sum_{\sigma\in G}\chi(\sigma)^{-1}\ord_{\mathfrak{P}}(\sigma(u_{\mathfrak{p}}))\neq 0.

Then

(104)

\frac{\sum_{\sigma\in G}\chi(\sigma)^{-1}\log_{p}(\Norm_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\sigma(u)))}{\chi(\ord_{G}(u))}=-\frac{L_{p}^{\prime}(\chi^{-1}\omega,0)}{L(\chi^{-1},0)}.

When applied to the Brumer–Stark unit
u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}
, this result can be interpreted as follows:

Corollary 6.3
.

We have

(105)

\Theta_{H}^{\prime}=-\sum_{\sigma\in G}\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(\sigma)^{-1}\log_{p}(\Norm_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\sigma(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}))).

Proof.

It is enough to show that the two sides agree after the application of
\chi
for every odd character
\chi
of
G
, i.e. that

(106)

L_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\prime}(\chi^{-1}\omega,0)=-\sum_{\sigma\in G}\chi(\sigma)^{-1}\log_{p}(\Norm_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\sigma(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}))).

Noting that

\frac{L_{p}^{\prime}(\chi^{-1}\omega,0)}{L(\chi^{-1},0)}=\frac{L_{p,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{\prime}(\chi^{-1}\omega,0)}{L_{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}(\chi^{-1},0)}

since the smoothing factors
\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\mid\Sigma^{\prime}}(1-\chi^{-1}(\sigma_{\mathfrak{q}})\mathrm{N}\mathfrak{q})
cancel, and recalling that

\chi(\ord_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}))=L_{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}(\chi^{-1},0)

by the definition of
u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}},
the desired result (
106
) follows directly from (
104
).
∎

The following result is known to the experts and has at its heart two deep facts—Theorem
6.2
above and the celebrated transcendence result of Brumer–Baker on the linear independence of logarithms of algebraic numbers over
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
\chi
be an odd character of
G
. We have
L_{p}^{\prime}(\chi\omega,0)\neq 0
.

Proof.

In view of Theorem
6.2
, it suffices to prove that

\sum_{\sigma\in G}\chi(\sigma)^{-1}\log_{p}(\Norm_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\sigma(u)))\neq 0

for any
u\in U_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-}
such that
\chi(\ord_{G}(u))\neq 0
. This follows from the theorem of Brumer–Baker
[
bb
]
as explained by Gross in
[
gross
]
*Proposition 2.13.
∎

We interpret this result in terms of the group-ring element
\Theta_{H}^{\prime}
as follows.

Corollary 6.5
.

The element
\Theta_{H}^{\prime}\in\overline{R}=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[G]^{-}
is a non-zerodivisor.

6.3.
The ring
R_{X,m}
and module
\nabla_{X,m}

Recall
R=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\mathfrak{g}]^{-}
. For any nonnegative integer
m
, define the ring

(107)

R_{X,m}=R[X]/(\Theta_{L}-\Theta_{H}^{\prime}X,X^{2},IX,I^{2},p^{m}X).

Lemma 6.6
.

For
m
sufficiently large, the canonical map
R/I^{2}\longrightarrow R_{X,m}
is injective.

Proof.

The proof is easier than Theorem
3.4
since
\Theta_{H}^{\prime}
is a non-zerodivisor in
\overline{R}
.
Let
a\in R
have image in
R_{X,m}
that vanishes. Writing down the fact that
a
lies in the ideal defining
R_{X,m}
shows that
a\equiv r\Theta_{L}\pmod{I^{2}}
for some
r\in R
such that
\overline{r}\Theta_{H}^{\prime}\equiv 0\pmod{p^{m}}
in
\overline{R}
. Yet since
\Theta_{H}^{\prime}
is a non-zerodivisor,
there exists a nonnegative integer
h
such that
\Theta_{H}^{\prime}
divides
p^{h}
in
\overline{R}
. We therefore find
p^{h}\overline{r}\equiv 0\pmod{p^{m}}
, whence
\overline{r}\equiv 0\pmod{p^{m-h}}
for
m\geq h
. Therefore
r\in(I,p^{m-h})
so
a\in(I^{2},p^{m-h}I)
. But
\#\Gamma
annihilates
I/I^{2}
(recall
\Gamma={\rm Gal}(L/H)
) so for
m
large enough
p^{m-h}I\subset I^{2}
, and we have
a\in I^{2}
as desired.
∎

Recall that for an integer
m\geq 0
,
F_{m}
denotes the
m
th layer of the cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension of
F
.
Let
\mathfrak{g}_{m}={\rm Gal}(LF_{m}/F)
and
\Gamma_{m}={\rm Gal}(LF_{m}/H)
. Let
R_{m}=\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\mathfrak{g}_{m}]^{-}
and
let

\boldsymbol{\psi}_{m}\colon\mathfrak{g}_{m}\longrightarrow R_{m}^{*}

denote the canonical character. We define a ring homomorphism

(108)

\beta_{m}\colon R_{m}\longrightarrow R_{X,m},\qquad\sigma\mapsto\sigma|_{L}+\sigma|_{H}\cdot\log_{p}(\varepsilon_{\cyc}(\sigma))X.

This is well-defined since the image of
\sigma\in\mathfrak{g}_{m}
determines the value of
\log_{p}(\varepsilon_{\cyc}(\sigma))
modulo
p^{m}
.

Define

(109)

\nabla_{X,m}={\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(LF_{m})_{R_{m}}\otimes_{R_{m}}R_{X,m},

where the
R_{m}
-action on the right factor is given by
\beta_{m}
.

Lemma 6.7
.

The
R_{X,m}
-module
\nabla_{X,m}
is quadratically presented and

\Fitt_{R_{X,m}}(\nabla_{X,m})=(\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})-\Theta_{L}).

To prepare for the proof of Lemma
6.7
,
define
I_{m}=\ker(\mathbf{Z}_{p}[\mathfrak{g}_{m}]^{-}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}_{p}[G]^{-})
. We have
I_{m}/I_{m}^{2}\cong\mathbf{Z}_{p}[G]^{-}\otimes\Gamma_{m}
. We lift the map
\rec_{G}
defined in (
6
) by defining

\rec_{G,m}\colon(\mathcal{O}_{H,\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}^{*})_{\overline{R}}\longrightarrow I_{m}/I_{m}^{2},\qquad\epsilon\mapsto\sum_{\sigma\in G}(\rec_{\mathfrak{P},m}(\sigma(\epsilon))-1)\tilde{\sigma}_{m}^{-1},

where
\rec_{\mathfrak{P},m}\colon H_{\mathfrak{P}}^{*}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{m}
is the reciprocity map and
\tilde{\sigma}_{m}
is a lift of
\sigma
in
\mathfrak{g}_{m}
. Under the projection
\mathfrak{g}_{m}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}
, the element
\rec_{\mathfrak{P},m}(\epsilon)
is mapped to
\rec_{\mathfrak{P}}(\epsilon)
. Furthermore,

\varepsilon_{\cyc}(\rec_{\mathfrak{P},m}(\epsilon))\equiv\Norm_{H_{\mathfrak{P}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\epsilon)\text{ in }(\mathbf{Z}/p^{m}\mathbf{Z})^{*}.

Therefore, under the map
\beta_{m}
defined in (
108
), we have

(110)

\beta_{m}(\rec_{G,m}(\epsilon))=\rec_{G}(\epsilon)+X\sum_{\sigma\in G}\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(\sigma)^{-1}\log_{p}\Norm_{H_{\mathfrak{P}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\sigma(\epsilon)).

Proof of Lemma
6.7
.

Lemmas
4.1
and
4.10
(applied to
LF_{m}
in place of
L
) imply that

{\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(LF_{m})_{R_{m}}
is quadratically presented over
R_{m}
and that

\Fitt_{R_{m}}({\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(LF_{m})_{R_{m}})=(\rec_{G,m}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})).

Applying (
110
), we then calculate

\displaystyle\beta_{m}(\rec_{G,m}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})+X\sum_{\sigma\in G}\overline{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(\sigma)^{-1}\log_{p}\Norm_{H_{\mathfrak{P}}/\mathbf{Q}_{p}}(\sigma(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}}))

(111)

\displaystyle=\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})-\Theta_{H}^{\prime}X

(112)

\displaystyle=\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})-\Theta_{L}.

Equation (
111
) follows from Corollary
6.3
and equation (
112
) is one of the defining relations of
R_{X,m}
in (
107
). The lemma follows.
∎

On the other hand, in our previous work
[
dk
]
we calculated the Fitting ideal of
{\nabla}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(LF_{m})
exactly, yielding the following result:

Theorem 6.8
.

We have
\Fitt_{R_{X,m}}(\nabla_{X,m})=0
.

Proof.

Theorem 3.3 of
[
dk
]
, recalled already in Theorem
4.2
above,
implies that

\Fitt_{R_{m}}(\nabla_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\Sigma^{\prime}}(LF_{m})_{R_{m}})=(\Theta^{LF_{m}}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}).

Passing to
R_{X,m}
by applying
\beta_{m}
, we find

\displaystyle\beta_{m}(\Theta^{LF_{m}}_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\Theta_{L}+X\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{g}_{m}}\zeta_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\sigma,0)\log_{p}\varepsilon_{\cyc}(\sigma)\overline{\sigma}^{-1}

(113)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{L}+X\sum_{\sigma\in G_{m}}\zeta_{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}},\Sigma^{\prime}}(\sigma,0)\log_{p}\varepsilon_{\cyc}(\sigma)\overline{\sigma}^{-1}

(114)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{L}-X\Theta_{H}^{\prime}

(115)

\displaystyle=0.

Here
\overline{\sigma}
denotes the image of
\sigma
in
G
. Equation (
113
) follows from the distribution property of partial zeta functions since
\log_{p}\varepsilon_{\cyc}(\sigma)\overline{\sigma}^{-1}
depends only on the image of
\sigma
in
G_{m}
. Equation (
114
) follows from Lemma
6.1
. Equation (
115
) is a defining relation of
R_{X,m}
. The result follows.
∎

Combining Lemma
6.7
with Theorem
6.8
, we obtain

(116)

\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})-\Theta_{L}=0\text{ in }R_{X,m}.

By Lemma
6.6
, equation (
116
) for
m
large enough yields

\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}})\equiv\Theta_{L}\pmod{I^{2}}

in
R
. This completes the proof of the
p
-part of the modified Gross conjecture (
20
) in Case 2(c).
Lemma
3.1
then yields the
p
-part of Gross’s conjecture for the Brumer–Stark unit
u_{\mathfrak{p}}
:

\rec_{G}(u_{\mathfrak{p}})\equiv\Theta_{S_{\mathfrak{p}},T}^{L/F}\pmod{I^{2}}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
